The brief you actually read is fine — the cloud app generates it against the local model and stores it in the database; today's was written at 00:21 ET and /api/health has been reporting it green throughout. The dead one is morning_briefing.py: a separate job that writes a markdown note into a git-tracked vault and posts a summary to Slack. Losing it costs the Slack summary and the vault note, not the brief itself.

What does hold up

All six compile under the python their plists name. dotenv and anthropic are installed for it. integrations/ is present next to the scripts, and chat/adapters and chat/core are present for the chat server. So the imports are fine and the code is intact.

What does not

The vault is computed from the script's own location.

VAULT = Path(__file__).parents[2] / "vault"

That is the whole problem. Moving the scripts moves the vault with them:

Script atVAULT resolves toExists?
the old worktree…/naughty-fermat-4efa9a/vaultno emptied with the worktree
~/.claude/scripts (after a repoint)/Users/grayson/vaultno never existed
—the real Obsidian vault in iCloudyes but nothing points at it

So a repointed morning brief would run, succeed, and write /Users/grayson/vault/10-Daily/<date>.md into a directory it creates for the purpose — which nobody opens, and which is not the Obsidian vault. It would look fixed and deliver nothing. That is the same shape as every other fault in this document.

The vault it wants was never really there. Exactly one vault file is tracked in the home repo — cleetus/.claude/worktrees/vault/10-Daily/2026-04-23.md — and it is deleted in the working tree right now. The docstring says it "commits vault", so this was meant to be a git-tracked markdown vault, separate from Obsidian. It has one day in it, from 23 April.

CLEETUS_ROOT points somewhere empty. The plists set it to /Users/grayson/cleetus, which contains only the hollow worktree and a .DS_Store. The scripts do load_dotenv(Path(os.environ["CLEETUS_ROOT"]) / ".env") and there is no .env there. load_dotenv does not complain about a missing file — it loads nothing and returns.

Keys are half there. ANTHROPIC_API_KEY is in the 90-key mirror. ALPHA_VANTAGE_KEY and STOCK_TICKERS are not — though that section degrades to a polite "key not set" line rather than crashing, and the tickers have a default.

So what would actually revive it

  1. Repoint ProgramArguments to ~/.claude/ (section 14).
  2. Decide where its vault lives, and make parents[2]/vault land there — a ~/vault symlink, or an edit to the script. If that target is the Obsidian vault, expect it to hang: iCloud does not serve launchd agents, which is exactly why cleetusd's own memory was moved to ~/cleetus-memory. A git-tracked ~/vault avoids that entirely and matches what the script was written to do.
  3. Put a .env at $CLEETUS_ROOT with at least ANTHROPIC_API_KEY, or point CLEETUS_ROOT at a directory that has one.
  4. Then read one brief before trusting the schedule.

Why this was worth an hour

The tempting move was to run six sed commands, watch the doctor go green, and report the morning brief restored. It would have gone green. The brief still would not have arrived, and the next person would have inherited a system that claims to be healthy — which is worse than the honest red it shows today.

19The flight map was dark, and nothing said so

Making the doctor stop skipping one check found that the flight map had been returning {"ok":false,"error":"no_adsb_feed_reachable"} in production. Every component in the chain was behaving correctly.

How a check that always skipped hid it

The flights check needed a session cookie the doctor did not have, so it skipped — every run, for as long as it has existed. A check that always skips is the same as no check. The site takes a password login that cloud_api had been using all along; borrowing it costs one request, and the check went red the first time it could actually run.

An empty answer is not an answer

{"ac":[], "msg": "No error", "total": 0}

That is adsb.lol, HTTP 200, for every anchor on earth. Perfectly well-formed and completely empty. The source loop read:

if (Array.isArray(ac)) return { source: name, aircraft: normalise(ac) };

[] is an Array. So the first source won with nothing, the fallbacks were never reached, and the map went dark — while every part of the chain reported success in its own terms. The sweeper "succeeded" with 0 aircraft. The ingest correctly refused an empty sweep. The endpoint said no_adsb_feed_reachable, which was true and named none of this.

Where the aircraft went

SourceStatus
adsb.lolempty 200 OK, "No error", zero aircraft over Atlanta at midday
airplanes.live403 "please contact us" — public access closed
adsb.fiworks real traffic, under the key aircraft rather than ac

adsb.fi is now tried first. The other two are kept rather than deleted — feeds come back, and a dead one costs a single failed request to check. A source now only wins when it actually produced aircraft, and a reachable-but-empty source is remembered separately from nobody answering, because a genuinely empty sky exists at 3am mid-Pacific and should not read as total failure.

Result

before   ok:false   error: no_adsb_feed_reachable
after    ok:true    4,862 aircraft   20/20 anchors   swept_by: mac-studio

Doctor: 24 checks, all cloud checks green. Test suite: 12 files, 39 tests, including one that fails if anyone reintroduces if (Array.isArray(ac)) return.

My own probe made the same mistake

Testing the three feeds by hand, I counted d["ac"] and reported adsb.fi as returning 0 aircraft too. It was returning plenty — under aircraft. Had I trusted that probe, the conclusion would have been "all three feeds are dead, nothing to be done". Dumping the raw body instead of the parsed count is what found it.

20Two Cleetuses, different instructions

cleetusd reads the agent briefs off the disk. The web app fetches them as static assets from the deployed site. Nobody had checked that those were the same text.

They were not

skin     disk 2,736 chars   live   903
deals    disk 4,139 chars   live   813
brief    disk 3,065 chars   live   621
fashion  disk 2,669 chars   live   899

Those live figures are exactly the pre-training sizes. The Opus 5 pass rewrote all 18 briefs on disk and they were never committed, so every specialist in the web app has been answering from the old short brief while the same specialist in the deck used the new one. Both halves work perfectly from their own point of view, which is why nothing said anything. One commit of brain/agents/ fixes it — and auto-deploys, so it is a deliberate act.

The doctor now samples four briefs and compares disk against what the site serves.

Outlook: the answer was in the same object

GET /api/microsoft/mail  →  {"ok":true,"connected":false,"messages":[]}

The health check graded ms.ok — which means "the request succeeded" — and reported outlook: connected. The field that answers the actual question, connected: false, was sitting in the same response being ignored. The endpoint returns that shape deliberately so the dashboard can show a "Connect Outlook" prompt; only the health check was reading the wrong field. Outlook mail has not been working, and the pill has been green.

Push: the same shape again

push reported ok:true, "phone registered" while its own last field said {note: "no_devices", sent: 0} — the most recent real push reached nobody. It was grading registration while the answer to delivery sat beside it. Now a push that has not been attempted lately is fine; one that was attempted and reached zero devices is not.

Calendar and mail, counted

google was graded Array.isArray(cal) — the same test that let the flight map die, since [] passes it. An empty calendar is legitimate, so it still counts as green, but the detail now reads "connected, 20 events" rather than just "connected". Reading "connected, 0 events" on a day you know you had meetings is the difference.

The pattern, stated plainly

Four times now the truth has been present in the same object as the lie: {ok:true, connected:false}, {registered, sent:0}, {"msg":"No error","total":0}, {ok:true, error:"no_adsb_feed_reachable"}. Nothing was hidden. Each check simply graded the field that was easy to reach instead of the field that answered the question.

The cloud app's eight integration checks are also pulled into the doctor now (28 checks), because a red pill on a page nobody has open is not a signal. It reads green today only because production still runs the old health.js; the fix is uncommitted, and outlook goes red when it ships.

21Finishing the integration sweep

Four integrations had been checked. These are the rest, verified by asking for real data rather than trusting the word "connected".

IntegrationAsked forGot
Google Calendarevents20
Gmailunread6 — matches "six emails waiting" in today's brief
Outlookunreadconnected:false section 20
Plaidaccounts2, ready
Schwabbalances3
Snapshotsnet worth historyreal series
Ledger P&L2026 by entity12 of 342 rows see below

The P&L describes 3% of the year

rowCount  342
untagged  {count: 330, moneyIn: 17833.39, moneyOut: 27310.43}

330 of 342 ledger rows belong to no entity — roughly $45,000 of money movement sitting outside the P&L. The tagged figures (Creo income $8,601, personal expenses $2,151) are correct and describe about 3% of the year.

This is not a bug. The endpoint reports untagged at the top level, honestly, and the books agent is already instructed that "a P&L reported without that percentage is a lie by omission". It is bookkeeping left to do, not code to fix.

What was wrong is that the tax agent had no such rule, and tax is where quoting an incomplete P&L does real damage: a quarterly estimate sized off 3% of the year is not conservative, it is wrong in the direction that underpays. Its brief now states the current numbers and requires the untagged figure alongside any P&L number. Asked for a quarterly estimate afterwards, it led with the gap and asked for the 2024 safe-harbor figure instead of quoting something false.

Eight briefs referenced a tool that no longer exists

Replacing browse with the web_* primitives (section 17) left nine mentions of browse across fashion, deals, music, hair, skin, poker, stocks and tax — instructions to call something no longer registered. Aliasing would have been the wrong fix, because the arguments differ: browse took a plain-English instruction, web_open takes a URL. Each mention was rewritten in the sense it was written.

The lesson became a check rather than a note. Backticked snake_case is the convention in these files for naming a tool, so the doctor now extracts every one and asserts it resolves in the registry — 19 briefs, all clean. Verified by putting a dead name back in stocks.md and watching it go red, because a check that has never failed has not been tested.

22What the agents do when they do not know

Per-agent memory was tested end to end and works exactly as designed. Testing it turned up something worse than a broken feature: a confident invention.

Memory: verified

Told the skin agent a fact, then asked three agents about it cold. The skin agent recalled it, the generalist knew it, an unrelated specialist did not. The skin agent also wrote it into the matching prompt in 40-Areas/Health/body.md, which is how the dossiers are meant to fill in over time. Every trace of the test fact was then removed from the vault, agent memory and the run log.

The unrelated agent did not say "I don't know". It made something up.

Asked about the same invented product name, the nutrition agent replied that it was a memory testing utility for x86 systems in the late 1990s, booted into DOS or minimal Linux, run overnight to catch bad RAM, since superseded by MemTest86+. Fluent, specific, entirely fabricated, no tool calls, no hedge.

The prompt was partly responsible. "No corporate hedging, no filler, no disclaimers" reads as an instruction never to admit ignorance, and the model resolved the conflict by producing something that sounded right. The two rules now sit next to each other and the conflict is settled in the text:

If you do not know something, say so in one short sentence and stop... Never fill
the gap with something that merely sounds right; a confident invention is the
single worst thing you can hand him, because he will act on it. "No hedging"
means do not pad an answer you actually have. It is not permission to
manufacture one you do not.

Retested with two invented terms against nutrition and tax. Both declined cleanly.

And the opposite fault, in the same breath

Declining honestly, the tax agent added that it "cannot access the Georgia DOR website" — while holding web_open, carrying a brief that tells it to verify rates against dor.georgia.gov. Refusing a capability it has is the same failure as refusing to read a file, and it was going unrecorded because looksFailed had no words for browsing. It does now, with tests both ways: refusing to browse counts as a failure, admitting genuine ignorance does not. That second test matters — grading an honest "I don't know" as a failure would send it to the cloud teacher to be fixed and train the behaviour straight back out.

Three services would not have come back

The doctor caught the air trackpad missing entirely, with an orphaned ffmpeg still holding its camera. That led to the policy behind it: airpad, cleetusd and web all carried KeepAlive: {SuccessfulExit: false}, which means launchd will not restart them after a clean exit. For a daemon that should never exit, that is a silent permanent death. Now unconditional, which adds no crash-loop risk because a failing service restarted under either policy.

Two self-inflicted faults while fixing it

launchctl bootout is asynchronous. Bootstrapping straight after it silently does nothing, and cleetusd and airpad sat down until the second attempt — wait for the service to leave the domain before loading it again. And PlistBuddy rewrites the whole file, dropping every XML comment: the notes explaining why each service exists and how to restart it were gone in one command. Restored by editing the original text instead.

Plaid, an hour apart

19:50   /api/plaid/accounts   ready, 2 accounts
21:00   /api/plaid/accounts   {"ready":false,"reason":"no_banks"}
21:00   /api/plaid/spending   ready, in $4,276 / out $3,371

The link is alive — spending returns real transactions. It is the accounts endpoint specifically, reporting no checking or debit accounts on the linked institutions. Caught only because the cloud app's own health is now pulled into the doctor. Whether it is a transient balance refresh or an item needing re-link is Grayson's to judge; the point is that it changed within the hour and something noticed.

A cold doctor pass runs in 2.3s across 29 checks, comfortably inside the deck's 45s abort.

23The half he talks to could not send email

Asked how email should work, Grayson picked "send freely to anyone" over draft-and-approve. The cloud endpoint was built for exactly that. The local Cleetus — the one with the disk, the shell and the cameras, the one he actually talks to — had no send tool at all. The decision was made and the capability was built, on opposite sides of the system.

send_email

Calls /api/google/send, which sends as him for real, logs every message to sent_emails before the call so a mistake is findable afterwards, and has one kill switch, EMAIL_SEND_ENABLED=0. Outlook cannot send at all: that grant is Mail.ReadWrite, which does not cover it.

No confirmation step in the tool, deliberately. cleetusd is already the most privileged thing on this machine; a prompt here would only train the model to answer its own prompt. The real protections are the session, the record written before the send, and a switch that works without a deploy. Reproducing them badly in the tool would make the system look safer while changing nothing.

One guard that is worth its weight: an address with no @ in it is refused by name. The realistic failure is the model writing "Isaiah" where the address goes, and that fails in a way nobody sees.

Verified without sending anything

POST /api/google/send  {to:"", subject:"", body:""}  →  {"ok":false,"error":"no_recipient"}

Which proves the session carried, the route exists, and the kill switch is not engaged — everything except the final Gmail call. No test email was sent. The first one should be his, knowingly, not a surprise in his sent folder.

A conflict for Grayson to settle

brain/agents/writing.md says, in two places:

Nothing goes out until he says "send it."
Never send, reply, or post anything. Draft only, every time, no exception
for "he obviously wants this sent."

That brief predates the "send freely" decision and directly contradicts it. It has NOT been changed, because the two readings lead to different behaviour and only he knows which he meant: the decision may have been about what the system should be capable of, while the writing agent specifically stays a drafter. Asked to send a test message, the writing agent refused and cited its own brief — capability present, policy declining, which is a coherent place to leave it. Every other agent will send when asked.

Also this round

Plaid recovered on its own: ready=false, no_banks at 21:00, ready=true with two accounts by 22:00. A genuine hour-long transient, seen only because the cloud app's health is now inside the doctor.

19 tools, 46 tests.

24The camera that was working the whole time

"The camera doesn't work" turned out to be four separate faults stacked on top of each other, and every one of them presented as nothing happening while every readout stayed green. It is the best example in this whole document of why a health field that cannot go red is worse than no health field.

It was pointing at the television

config.json had display: null, and null meant the first non-main display. That is display 3 — the LG. So the pointer was being driven correctly, at full rate, on a screen nobody was looking at. Nothing was broken and nothing reported a fault. null now means the main display, and the picker names screens by EDID vendor, because two of the three here are 1920×1080 and "1920×1080" identifies neither.

The tracker thread had been dead for hours

MediaPipe's video mode rejects a timestamp that is not strictly increasing — by raising, on the calling thread. It was being fed int(time.time()*1000), a wall clock that steps backwards whenever ntpd corrects it. One correction, thread gone.

Everything downstream carried on looking healthy, which is the part worth remembering: the camera reader is a different thread, the HTTP server is another, and the last annotated frame stays in memory, so the live view kept serving it. A frozen JPEG is indistinguishable from a very still room. The loop is supervised now, the clock is monotonic, and the timestamp is a counter that cannot repeat.

The camera was sending one picture over and over

Of 68 consecutive frames pulled off the wire, one was distinct. Frame counters read 70fps, ffmpeg sat at 1200% CPU, the multipart stream was byte-perfect, 434 frames arrived in six seconds. All true. All irrelevant.

The C920 advertises exactly 30.000030 fps. Ask for 30, or let ffmpeg default to 29.97, and avfoundation pads the stream with duplicate frames rather than admitting it cannot serve the rate. Asking for the exact value gives 100% unique frames and drops ffmpeg to 10% CPU. The original setting made it worse still: uncompressed 1080p is ~124 MB/s and this is a USB 2.0 device, which measured 0.2 real frames per second.

capturereal fps
1920×1080 uncompressed (as configured)0.2
1280×7205.2
864×480 at 30.00003019

Resolution barely moves the rate, which was the surprise — 432×240 and 864×480 both give 19. Room light was tested and refuted (Litra off 19.3, full 19.0), as was contention with the BRIO, which is on a different USB bus. The camera has simply negotiated its 20fps mode. Past that needs manual UVC exposure control or a different camera; the BRIO measures 27.

A polite restart could not fix it

The watchdog tested that _seq advances — and duplicates advance _seq, so the check meant to catch a frozen stream was blind to the way this one freezes. It now judges content as a rate: a stuck capture still emits one genuinely new frame a second, so any consecutive-identical test resets on that frame and never fires. Hundreds delivered against a handful distinct is what is unambiguous.

And its restart did not work. _restart_proc sent SIGTERM; ffmpeg spinning on avfoundation does not unwind politely, so the USB session was still half-open when the replacement opened the device and inherited the same wedged mode. SIGKILL, and two seconds before reopening. Verified live: stuck at 11–24 distinct and 1200% CPU, self-healed to 49 of 51 distinct at 9.6%.

The habit this leaves behind

Count distinct frames, never delivered frames. real_fps in /api/state counts content changes and fps counts arrivals; when they disagree, believe the smaller one. Every fault above was visible in that one ratio and invisible in everything else.

Calibration

The mapping trimmed a fixed 18% off each frame edge and stretched the rest over the screen, which assumes the box you gesture in is centred and square-on to the lens. Neither is true: he sits low and left of centre, and the camera sees the plane at an angle, so the swept rectangle arrives as a trapezoid. /calibrate fits a homography from four measured corners — an affine transform cannot do it, the two perspective terms are the point. It measures his reach, not the screen, so one calibration holds whether the cursor drives one display or all three. Measured reach: 56% × 31% of frame.

Scrolling is calibrated separately, because it is a different gesture measured against different things: a hold to find the jitter he cannot help, a sweep to find how far he can comfortably reach. It was previously measured in screen pixels, so the same movement scrolled a different amount on the television than on the portrait panel. Normalised now, with gain applied once at the end.

Also: the tracker was handing MediaPipe the full frame when the landmarker works on ~256px, so a full-size colour conversion and an extra resize were being paid for nothing. It gets a 384px copy; the overlay keeps the full frame. Tracking went from 14 delivered / 12 distinct to 28 / 22.

25Two pages, and an answer that stopped mid-sentence

The dashboard with the trackpad, the light and the agents on it existed only at 127.0.0.1:8767. That is not a page on the site: no link to it, nothing to open from a phone, and it disappeared the moment he left the desk.

/reach

Read-only state travels through /api/reach, which holds the bearer server-side and forwards over the existing tunnel, so the page works from anywhere. Anything that moves the cursor does not: those routes go straight to 127.0.0.1:8767, and cleetusd refuses them for any request carrying forwarding headers — so a valid token arriving through the tunnel is turned away too. Verified against the live tunnel with a real token: not_local.

The middleware lifts three CSP rules for that path alone. The one that matters is upgrade-insecure-requests, which rewrites http://127.0.0.1 to https:// — cleetusd does not speak it, and the failure is invisible: no violation to read, the request simply never arrives. The same rule had been silently blocking the deck's own probe of cleetusd on every load, which is why the "open full Cleetus" link never appeared even sitting at the Mac.

/ruview

RuView reads presence, breathing and heart rate out of the disturbance a body makes in ambient WiFi. The band-1 left screen was the sun's altitude; it is the house now — same rings, because the reference puts a ring target there, but they are node range instead of orbits and the dot that moves is a person.

Every field is read off the real API, taken from the sensing server's handlers rather than guessed, and the readable endpoints are an allowlist by exact name: the server also exposes training, model loading and recording, none of which should become reachable just because the read path did. Nothing is invented when the server is absent, which is the state it is in until the hardware exists — and that matters more here than anywhere else on the deck, because a plausible dot on a presence sensor is a claim that somebody is in the house.

The chat was answering with its own preamble

Answers stopped mid-thought, on a colon: "Let me check the Cleetus V2 project for GP Productions or any studio/location related code:" with fourteen tool calls of real work above it and no conclusion.

The loop assigned answer = res.text || answer every pass. This model narrates before it acts, so a turn that calls tools usually carries a line of text with them — a preamble, not an answer — and each one overwrote the last. Run out of steps and the final preamble is the result. Only a turn that calls no tools is an answer now.

The salvage pass meant to catch this could never fire: it was gated on the answer being empty, and it was never empty, it was half a sentence. And appending "now answer" to the transcript produced more narration, because after twenty tool calls the context is a tool loop. It builds a fresh two-message prompt from the findings instead, at lower temperature, and asks again if the reply still ends on a promise.

looksFailed returned false for all of these — used.length > 0 meant "it did some work" and exited early — so the one failure a user actually complains about was the only one the teacher never saw. Same question, same 20 tool calls: 378 characters ending on a colon, against 1,479 that lead with the conclusion and name the paths.

A specialist for the desk

New studio agent, alongside the builder. It owns the trackpad, the cameras, the light and the desk watcher, and its brief is mostly the habits above: measure before theorising, count distinct frames, ask the device what it supports, never trust a field that is only assigned once.

26The doctor was reading the lying number

Section 24 established that fps counts arrivals and real_fps counts content changes, and that avfoundation pads a stream it cannot serve. The doctor was asserting fps > 20 — the number that cannot tell a frozen camera from a still room, against a threshold this hardware can never reach.

CheckAsserts
camera producing NEW framesdelivered ÷ distinct < 2. The gap between them is the fault signature
frame rate usabledistinct ≥ 6/s. The old bar of 20 was unreachable: measured ceiling is ~19
tracker thread alivelive and no tracker_error — it once died for hours while every readout stayed green
can move the cursorAccessibility still granted

Reading now: 13.1 distinct/s of 18.2 delivered/s, tracker live, accessibility granted. 32 checks total.

The tool-name check earned itself

It went red on the new studio.md brief, for real_fps — a JSON field, not a tool. Correct catch, wrong conclusion, so it joins untagged on a deliberately short allowlist. If that list starts growing, the backtick convention has stopped meaning anything and the check should be rethought rather than padded.

Plaid is flapping, not broken

19:50  ready, 2 accounts
21:00  ready=false, no_banks
22:00  ready, 2 accounts
22:55  ready=false, no_banks   (three reads over 40s, all the same)

The link itself stays alive throughout — /api/plaid/spending returns real transactions in every one of those windows. It is the accounts endpoint specifically, and it is intermittent across hours rather than down. Worth knowing before anyone re-links an item that may not need it.

Two sessions, one system

Sections 24 and 25 were written by another session working on this machine at the same time as this one. Both sets of changes to src/agent.mjs survived intact — their preamble fix and this side's failure heuristics coexist, 19 tools and 46 tests all passing — but that was luck rather than design. Anyone working here should check what has changed under them before assuming their picture of the system is current.

27Why Plaid kept saying you have no bank accounts

The flapping in section 26 has a cause, and it is one line.

const bd = await br.json();
if (!br.ok) continue;        // ← every Plaid failure, silently skipped

When Plaid's balance call failed for an institution, the loop moved on. When it failed for every institution, the list came out empty and the endpoint answered:

{"ready":false,"reason":"no_banks",
 "message":"No checking/debit accounts found on the linked institutions yet."}

That is a statement about his bank. The truth was that Plaid had a bad minute. Same shape as the flight map answering total: 0 and the tax P&L describing 3% of the year: a well-formed reply that reads as fact and is actually an absence.

Three answers where there was one

SituationNow returnsWhat to do
Calls succeeded, no depository accountsno_banksa real statement about his banks
Calls failedupstream_error + the Plaid error codesusually nothing; it passes
Some answered, some did notready:true, partial:true, missing:[…]the total is real and WRONG

The third is the one that mattered most and did not exist before. A total missing one of two banks is a number he would act on, and it looked identical to a complete one. Plaid names its own faults precisely — ITEM_LOGIN_REQUIRED means re-link, RATE_LIMIT_EXCEEDED means wait — and those opposite instructions were both being flattened into "accounts not ready".

/api/health carried the same flattening and graded a partial total as connected. It now separates all five states, and a partial total is red.

Not deployed

Both files are edited and unpushed, like the rest of this session's cloud-side work. Pushing to main auto-deploys, so it stays Grayson's call. Nothing here changes what Plaid is asked for — only what the app says when Plaid does not answer.

28The front end, checked; and a hazard worth naming

Every page loads, and /body does what he asked for

Ten pages fetched with a real session: /, /body, /flights, /reach, /fitness, /nutrition, /timeline, /music, /space, /hub. All 200, all HTML, all correctly titled.

/body carries the three layers he described — wardrobe outside, skin and hair under it, nutrition and fitness beneath that — over a real SVG silhouette. Clicking a region calls pick(layer, agent), which selects that specialist and focuses the input with "Ask the skin agent…". The page is the destination, which is why a first check looking for links out of it found nothing and concluded wrongly. The three live endpoints it draws from (nutrition/diary, fitness/workout, outfit) all answer; the first two are empty today because nothing has been logged, which is a legitimate empty rather than a broken one — a distinction this document has spent a lot of pages on.

Two agents are editing this repo at once

Sections 24, 25 and five commits on main came from a different session working on this machine at the same time as this one. Their work and this session's coexist in ~/cleetusv2 right now: eighteen modified agent briefs and two modified API files from here, sitting uncommitted in a tree the other session commits from.

Nothing has gone wrong. Their commits are surgical — the most recent is one file, reach.html — and no work from this side has been swept into them. But main is currently ahead of origin by one commit, and pushing main auto-deploys. A git commit -a from either side would carry the other's uncommitted work into production, including the brief changes deliberately left for Grayson to decide on.

The uncommitted work is backed up as a patch at ~/cleetus-memory/pending/cleetusv2-uncommitted.patch (1,113 lines) and restores with git apply. That is insurance, not a fix. The fix is knowing that a shared checkout has two writers.

29A test that agreed with the code instead of reality

Section 22 widened the failure heuristic so that refusing to browse would count, now that browsing works. That widening quietly broke a perfectly good answer, and the unit test written alongside it passed the whole time.

The sentence it started failing

"I can browse Amazon and show you options/prices, but I don't have the
 ability to actually place purchases."

He can browse. He cannot buy. Both halves are true and the sentence is exactly what he should say. The check asked whether a refusal phrase and a capability word appeared anywhere in the answer, which is a different question, so "browse" in the first half condemned the refusal in the second.

Why the test did not catch it

The test for "an honest boundary is NOT a failure" used "I can't actually place purchases on Amazon, but I can compare prices for you" — which happens to contain no browsing word, so it passed before and after. The example was invented to match the code's assumption rather than taken from what the model actually said. A test written from the same premise as its subject confirms the premise. The replacements are verbatim model output.

The fix

The refusal and the capability must now be in the same clause. Splitting on "but" matters as much as splitting on full stops, because "I can X but I can't Y" is one sentence carrying two opposite claims and only the second is being graded. Eight cases, verbatim from real answers, now grade correctly in both directions.

And the record it had already poisoned

Fixing the grader does not rewrite history: the run file still said status: failed, so the improve loop's next three passes would have gone to a bug that never existed. Historical runs were re-graded with the corrected logic — exactly one was misgraded, the other three genuinely failed — and the corrected file carries a note saying why the verdict changed. A status that flips with no explanation is worse than a wrong one.

The loop now proposes the next real item instead: 8 candidates, top of the list an answer that genuinely came back empty.

Today's brief, verified live

Written 00:19 ET, fresh rather than cached, on laguna-xs-2.1:q8_0: served 76, fallbacks 0. The claim in section 01 held overnight, unattended. 48 tests.

30Three failures the loop could not see

Re-grading the runs marked failed was only half the job. The mirror question — which runs marked done would fail under the current grader — found three, and those were invisible to the improve loop entirely, because it looks for failures.

All three end on an announcement instead of an answer:

"Let me check the Cleetus V2 project for GP Productions or an…"
"So your health.md exists but is unfilled. Let me check if th…"
"Now let me look at the test structure to understand how test…"

Twelve tool calls of real work above each one, and no conclusion. They were recorded as done because the grader could not yet see it — endsOnAPromise came later, from the other session's work in section 25.

Correcting it without creating ghost work

Flipping them to failed makes the record honest and sends an automated loop hunting a bug that is already fixed — the exact waste section 29 removed in the other direction. Leaving them alone keeps the loop quiet and the record wrong.

Both, then: the status is corrected, each file carries a note saying why it changed and that the cause is fixed, and the three are pre-recorded in improve-state.json with their issue keys so the loop's already-attempted filter skips them. It now proposes a genuine failure — an answer that really did come back empty — from 8 candidates.

A stale mental model, caught by reading

One run looked impossible: twenty tool calls, a real answer, and still graded a failure, when looksFailed returns early on any run that used tools. The function had grown a line this session did not write — the other session's endsOnAPromise check, sitting above that early return. The model of the code was stale, not the code wrong. With two writers in one tree, reading beats remembering.

31The air trackpad: every threshold was a cliff nobody could see

The trackpad "did not work" in four different ways over one night, and all four were the same fault wearing different clothes: a number chosen by eye, checked against nobody's hand, guarding a behaviour that could not report being blocked.

What was actually wrong

SymptomCause
Scrolling stopped after half a secondOnly the point pose counted as engaged, so raising the second finger started the 0.6s release clock and handed control back mid-sweep.
Scrolling felt like a staircaseThe deadzone was a gate: it waited to be exceeded then released the WHOLE distance and re-centred. It is a band dragged behind the fingers now.
Long pages were hopelessGain was pixels-per-reach fitted against whichever display was driven, which means nothing spanning three. It is SCREENS per unit of reach now, converted per scroll against the display the pointer is on.
Slow sweeps did nothing at allint() truncation. 0.4px a frame became zero, every frame. The remainder is carried.
The pointer walked down the screen while scrollingMediaPipe drops "middle finger extended" for a frame or two mid-sweep; the code fell through to the pointer and warped the cursor to wherever the hand had travelled. The pose is latched now.
Scrolling worked one way onlyThe scroll signal clamped at 1.6, which this calibration reaches at frame y 0.85 — exactly where a hand rests. Moving down (page up) saturated instantly; moving up had the whole box.
The desktop would not come backThe gesture window measured from when a pose was ENTERED, so closing your fingers only counted within 1.2s of opening them. You spend longer than that looking at the desktop.
Clicking never firedThumb-to-index must fall below 0.55 and a real pinch on this hand reads about 1.1. Unreachable.

The shape of the whole class

From inside the process, "you are not pinching hard enough" and "you are not pinching" are the same observation. A threshold nobody can reach is invisible: nothing logs, nothing errors, every readout stays green. The fix is not a better default. It is measuring the hand and drawing the live value next to the line that judges it.

Two that were not thresholds at all

The camera was pointed at the back of his head. Frames arriving, all distinct, tracker live, fps healthy, and the hand never in shot. No readout can tell you where a lens is aimed — the only way to know is to pull a frame off the stream and look at it.

A hand too small to resolve produces a confident, complete, fictional skeleton. At about 8% of frame the landmarks measured fingertips nearer the wrist than their own knuckles and a pinch that read the same open and closed. The palm detector had been handed a 384px copy on the reasoning that MediaPipe resizes internally — true of the landmark model, false of the detector, which has to find the hand in the whole picture first. It gets the native 864 now; measured cost, none, because the camera was always the cap at 15 distinct fps.

Staying up

KeepAlive only catches a process that dies, and this one never did. Every real outage left it alive and green: a wedged tracking loop still answering /api/state, a stream corrupted by another app opening the camera, a driver padding frames with duplicates. A watchdog thread now checks the one honest signal — no fresh DISTINCT frame recently — and signals itself so launchd brings back a process that reaps the orphaned ffmpeg and rebuilds the graph. ProcessType is Interactive so the system stops throttling the thing the cursor is attached to.

The calibration was one truncated write from gone: config.json was rewritten by truncate-and-write from HTTP handler threads with no lock, and an unparseable config does not raise, it silently means "never calibrated". Writes are atomic and locked, and the last config carrying a calibration is kept beside it and recovered automatically. Drilled against a truncated file, an empty-but-valid one, and two concurrent writers.

Show desktop, on macOS 26

CoreDock.framework is gone and SkyLight exports no show-desktop call. F11 posts and works — measured, three of four windows parked off their display and posting again brought every one back. That is a different case from switching spaces, where a synthetic Ctrl+Arrow posts successfully and the window server ignores it. Because F11 is a toggle, direction is decided by asking the window server what is on screen rather than by remembering.

32Studio Locate: the page, and the agent that builds it

Everything above argued for one thing: a surface where the numbers are visible and the calibration happens where you are standing. That is /studio.

What is on it

The live C920 feed as the tracker sees it; every pose verdict as a flag; and three gauges that draw the live value against the threshold that judges it — pinch against the click line, grip against the two halves of the desktop gesture, index reach against the line that decides whether you are pointing at all. It reports how much of the frame the hand fills and says so when that drops under 14%, where the landmarks stop being trustworthy.

The four-pose fit runs in the page: it waits for a hand rather than counting down, samples each pose, refuses on measurements that cannot be real, and writes pointing, clicking and the desktop gesture through the calibration API with no restart. Arm/disarm, span, display targeting and scroll tuning are all there too.

The agent

A chat wired to cleetusd's /chat/stream with agent=studio — the local model on this Mac, with real file and shell tools, streaming each tool call as it makes it. Its brief (brain/agents/studio.md) now carries the whole project: every gesture, every threshold and what it gates, every trap in the table above, the full state schema, and the instruction that it changes code rather than describing changes. The live tracker state is attached to each message, because the agent is being asked about a hand in front of a camera it cannot see.

The security line, unchanged

Read-only state travels through /api/reach over the tunnel, so the page is worth opening from a phone. Anything that moves the cursor or writes a threshold goes straight to 127.0.0.1:8767 and only answers a request that originated on this Mac — a valid bearer token arriving through cloudflared is rejected, because the tunnel carries the bearer and cursor control of a machine holding bank credentials must not be reachable with it. Calibration joined that gate as a prefix, since the thresholds decide what the cursor does.

The door

The moon statistics tile on the deck became the way in: a rotating 3D scene of the actual problem — the desk as a perspective grid, the camera above it, the cone of what it can see, and a fingertip inside that cone dropping a shadow. Projected by hand, painter-ordered, no library. The moon maths is still in the file, unreferenced, so putting that panel back is markup and two calls.

33A camera hiccup killed the whole assistant

Three long chat calls died mid-request with a socket error. They had nothing in common with each other and nothing to do with the camera. cleetusd had exited.

Error [ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT]: Cannot write headers after they are sent
    at Server.<anonymous> (src/server.mjs:249)
Node.js v26.3.0

The airpad MJPEG proxy sends its 200, then pumps frames for as long as the browser watches. On any error during that pump it called res.writeHead(502) — reasonable-looking code, and impossible: the headers went out before the first frame. The second writeHead throws, and a throw inside an async handler is an unhandled rejection, which Node answers by exiting the process.

So a camera restarting took down the assistant, every conversation in flight with it, and the KeepAlive brought it back looking perfectly healthy. Reproduced deliberately: start consuming the stream, kickstart airpad underneath it, watch the daemon's run counter increment. After the fix the counter holds at 17 and /health stays 200 throughout.

Two fixes, one general

The proxy now distinguishes the two moments it can fail. Before the pump, nothing has been written and a 502 is honest. After, the headers are spent and the only correct move is to end the response — a video that stops is a video that stops, and the browser reconnects.

More usefully, json() is now safe to call after a response has begun, so every error path in the file inherits the guard rather than each one being audited. And createServer is no longer handed a bare async function: the handler is wrapped, so a fault returns 500 and gets logged loudly instead of being fatal to everything happening at the time. Surviving is only better than crashing if the fault still shows up.

The test that was wrong and still useful

The first version scanned every catch for a writeHead within 300 characters. It failed immediately — on a block whose only sin was calling json(), because the window ran past the end of the block. The detection was crude, but what it pointed at was real: json() called writeHead unguarded, and several error paths reach it mid-response. Fixing that centrally was better than the per-catch audit the test was asking for. 52 tests.

34The improve loop's work list, emptied honestly

Before it can be trusted to run unattended, its list has to contain only work that is actually still there. Every item on it was either fixed or a ghost.

Three open failures, re-run rather than assumed

The loop's top three came from run files. Each original question was asked again and graded with the current logic:

The questionThenNow
search my vault for anything about breakouts12 tools, empty answer17 tools, 821 chars
how much can you edit your own code and ship itrefused20 tools, 2,137 chars
can you fix studio locatestopped on an announcement20 tools, 1,101 chars

All three causes were genuinely fixed — the forced final pass, the preamble check, the anti-confabulation rule. They are recorded as verified-fixed with the evidence attached, because "I re-ran it and it worked" is a different claim from "I changed something and assume it worked". And cleetusd survived all three long requests without restarting, which it would not have done an hour earlier.

Five copies of one crash

The rest of the list was the error log: five near-identical ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT lines from the crash in section 33. A log line is evidence a bug existed, not that it exists now. They now dedupe by key, so five stack lines count as one bug rather than making the list look five times longer than it is, and the crash is recorded as verified-fixed against the live reproduction.

What is left

would fix:   /api/health reports plaid is down
candidates:  2
blockers:    working tree is dirty
             baseline is already red (plaid)

One real item, and the loop is refusing to act on it for two independent reasons — both correct. It will not build on uncommitted work, and it will not run against a red baseline because it could not then tell its own damage from the damage that was already there. The plaid reporting fix that explains that red is itself sitting uncommitted, which is why both blockers point at the same decision.

35The teacher works. The counter next to it did not.

The learning loop had never been audited end to end. It turns out to work — and the one number reporting on it was permanently zero.

Verified working

Skills went from 2 to 5 overnight without anyone asking for them. Two came from the real failures at 21:34 and 21:38: "Diagnose and fix a vague 'fix <service>' request", written against the studio-locate run, with a proper Use when and a numbered procedure. Claude wrote them because laguna failed, which is exactly the designed path.

Retrieval was tested against three questions and returned the right skill for each:

"can you fix studio locate"          → Diagnose and fix a vague 'fix <service>' request
"search my vault about my skin"     → Search the Obsidian vault for a topic keyword
"what did I lift today"             → Report today's workout and week-to-date

uses: 0, forever

Every skill carried uses: 0 from the moment it was written, and nothing in the codebase ever incremented it. A number that is permanently zero is worse than no number: it looks like a measurement, so a skill that had fired forty times still read "never useful" and invited deleting the ones that work.

Retrieval now bumps the count and stamps last_used, written fire-and-forget so a bookkeeping failure can never cost an answer. Verified by retrieving one skill twice and another once: 2, 1, and 0 on the untouched ones. It counts retrievals, not successes, and the field is named for what it measures — whether a skill actually helped is a harder question and this does not pretend to answer it.

The sweep was polluting what it measured

A "toolsweep probe" skill was sitting in the skills list looking like something Cleetus had learned. remember_fact and save_skill can only be tested by really writing, so the probes have to be real — but leaving them is not acceptable in a memory Grayson reads. bin/tools-check.mjs now removes its own probes, and reports what it deliberately does not exercise:

20 calls, 0 broken.
  not exercised: send_email — sends real email to a real person
cleaned up probes: toolsweep-probe.md, builder.md

Anything untested without a stated reason now exits non-zero, so a genuine gap cannot hide in that same line.

36Most questions never reached their specialist

The router picks which agent answers, and with it which brief, which per-agent memory and which dossiers get loaded. It had never been measured. It was getting it right 4 times in 12, and falling through to the generalist on seven of them.

That is the quiet kind of failure this document keeps finding. A question about breakouts answered without the skin brief and without the skin memory does not look broken. It looks like a normal answer that happens to be worse — and all the training, all the per-agent memory, every dossier, was being skipped on most messages.

Two causes, one of them pure loss

The menu made the generalist attractive. cleetus was listed alongside the specialists as "anything else, or general conversation", which reads like a legitimate choice rather than a last resort. It is now described as one, with an explicit instruction to prefer a specific agent.

The parser threw away right answers. Asked for a single id, the gate replied:

"\boxed{nutrition}"

The correct id, wrapped in LaTeX by a small model told to answer in one word. The parser lowercased and stripped every non-letter, producing boxednutrition, which is not an agent, so it fell back to the generalist. Small models decorate. The parser now looks for a known id inside the reply, longest first so one id cannot hide within another.

Measured again

before   4/12 matched   7/12 fell through to the generalist
after    8/12 matched   0/12 fell through

The four remaining disagreements are all between neighbouring specialists — "how much did I bench" went to muscle rather than fitness, "draft a reply to the venue about the deposit" went to booking rather than writing. Those are defensible readings, and the label was mine, not truth. The number that matters is the second one: every question now reaches a relevant specialist.

A rule earned three times over

The test asserting the generalist is no longer offered as a peer failed immediately — on the comment explaining that the phrase had been removed. That is the third source-scanning test this session to trip over prose quoting the very string it forbids. Comments are now stripped before matching, and the rule is written down: when asserting on source, assert on code, never on the prose sitting next to it.

37The check that could not catch the bug it was built for

Section 21 turned a lesson into a check: no brief may tell an agent to call a tool that does not exist. It went green for a week. It could never have gone red for the actual case.

How it fell over first

The other session's studio.md documents config keys and JSON fields the way every brief documents anything — in backticks. The check assumed backticked snake_case meant "tool" and produced fourteen false positives at once: engage_hold, pinch_close, tracker_error, cal_corners and the rest.

The allowlist was already at two entries with a note saying that if it started growing, the convention had stopped meaning anything and the check should be rethought rather than padded. It grew. So it was rethought: what separates a tool from a field is not its shape but its context. A tool is invoked — "through cloud_api", "come from web_open", "Run vault_search on the person". A field is named — "pinch_close / pinch_open — thumb-to-index".

Then the negative control failed, and that was the real find

Reintroducing browse into a brief — the genuine tool, genuinely removed, that genuinely left nine stale mentions — did not turn the check red.

/`([a-z][a-z0-9]*(?:_[a-z0-9]+)+)`/     ← requires an underscore

browse is one word. It could never match. The check has never been capable of catching the bug it was written for.

Worse, it had a passing negative control. That test used browse_the_web — invented to look like a tool name, and carrying the underscore the pattern needed. It went red, the check looked proven, and the thing it actually had to catch stayed invisible. Second time this session a test has passed for the wrong reason because the example was invented rather than taken from what really happened.

Now

The identifier pattern is loose and call-context does the filtering. Verified in both directions with the real string: clean on 20 briefs, red on browse (stocks) the moment it is put back, clean again when removed. 56 tests.

Also noted

studio-locate on :8765 is not answering. It belongs to the other session, which is actively working on it, so it has been left alone rather than restarted out from under them — but it is red in the doctor and worth a look if it stays that way.

38Checks that vanished instead of failing

Having found a check that could never fire, the obvious next question was which others could not. The answer was not "none".

Four checks disappeared whenever airpad did

Every airpad check sat behind if (pad.status === 200). With airpad down they were not evaluated, not reported, and not missed — the report simply came back four checks shorter and said "all clear" about what remained. A count that changes silently is the same failure as a check that always skips: nothing distinguishes "this passed" from "this was never asked".

The same gate existed on cleetusd's own internals (behind /health answering) and on everything cloud-side (behind the site login). All of them now emit a named skip when their precondition fails, so the set is always the same length and an absence has a reason attached.

with airpad unreachable:
  --  camera producing NEW frames   airpad is not answering
  --  frame rate usable             airpad is not answering
  --  tracker thread alive          airpad is not answering
  --  can move the cursor           airpad is not answering
  --  CORS open for the dashboard   airpad is not answering
total checks: 32   (was 32 with airpad up)

Verified by running the real doctor against a dead port rather than trusting the branch, because an untested negative path is exactly what section 37 was about.

studio-locate has no launch agent

It is down, and it is the one service started by hand — so the doctor's advice, "check the launch agent for studio-locate", pointed at a file that does not exist. It now says what actually starts it. No agent was created for it: the other session is iterating on that service right now, and a KeepAlive agent would fight them for the process. That is a decision for whoever owns it, not a cleanup to do quietly underneath.

The rule this instrument keeps relearning

Three separate versions of one mistake, now: a check that always skipped (flights), a check that could not match its own target (tool names), and checks that vanished with their service (airpad). Each looked healthy from the outside. An instrument has to be able to say "I could not tell" — out loud, in the same shape as everything else. 59 tests.

39The service nothing was keeping alive

studio-locate had been down for an hour. It was the only Cleetus service with no launch agent — which is precisely why it was the one that was down.

It only ever ran when somebody started it by hand, and a hand-started process does not outlive the session that spawned it. The air trackpad taught that lesson three separate times before it got an agent; the browser harness taught it again in section 15. This was the last one still learning it.

It has com.cleetus.studio now, running the same command run.sh documents, with unconditional KeepAlive. Up on the first try: BRIO, real frames, all three cameras enumerated. The plist says how to take it back — launchctl bootout — because the other session works on that service and should not have to guess.

And then the trap, one hour after writing it down

The new plist was invalid XML. Its comment quoted the start command, which contains - -host and - -port, and a double hyphen is illegal inside an XML comment. Section 06 of this document lists that trap. It was written by the same session, earlier the same night, about the same two files.

launchd does not mind, which is what makes it dangerous: the service came up perfectly and only a strict parser objects. So the doctor now runs every com.cleetus.* plist through Python's plistlib, which is strict where PlistBuddy is not. It immediately found the two long-standing offenders:

FAIL every plist is valid XML   2 unparseable: flights, improve
 ok  every plist is valid XML   17 plists parse

All three are fixed by rephrasing the prose rather than quoting a command line into it.

A test that guarded a workaround

One test asserted that studio-locate had no agent and must not be told to restart one. That was true when written and became false the moment the real fix landed. It now asserts the opposite and stronger property: every service the doctor watches on a port has a launch agent in its list. A test written around a known gap should be rewritten when the gap closes, not deleted — the invariant was always "these two lists agree". 60 tests, 34 checks.

40Reasoning suppression is a cliff, not an optimisation

Section 06 lists "always think:false" as a cost lesson, worth a few hundred tokens. Measuring it properly showed the failure is worse than expensive: without the right flag, the answer comes back empty.

cleetusd, native API

think:false   8 tokens    0 chars reasoning   "The capital of France is Paris."
think:true  294 tokens 1332 chars reasoning   "The capital of France is Paris."

Honoured, as documented. Same answer, 286 tokens saved on a trivial question.

The web app, /v1 shim — a different story

Same question, same model, 200-token budget, through the tunnel the deployed app actually uses:

tokensreasoninganswer
no flag200 (capped)913 charsEMPTY
reasoning_effort:"none"80correct, 0.3s

The whole budget went to thinking and the model never wrote a word. That is not a slower answer, it is no answer, and the only other thing that would notice is the fallback counter climbing as every local call gets retried against Claude.

Both halves are doing the right thing today: cleetusd passes think:false on the native API, and the web app adds reasoning_effort:"none" whenever thinking is off, which is the default. Production agrees — 76 served, 0 fallbacks.

Made checkable

It hangs on one environment variable. LLM_THINK=1 removes the flag from every call the web app makes, and the symptom is blank answers rather than an error. The doctor now asks the model for a single word over the same /v1 path and fails if the reply is empty or carries any reasoning:

ok   model answers over /v1 without burning the budget   "ready" · 2 tokens · 0 chars reasoning

Verified in both directions — dropping the flag turns it red with EMPTY answer · 346 chars of reasoning, which is the whole point. 35 checks, 60 tests.

41Two places he cannot see, and did not mention

"Make sure Cleetus can access any file on my computer, always" was the instruction. check_access exists to answer that honestly. It was answering it incompletely.

The report named one denial. There are three.

It probed Library/Mail and reported it denied. Library/Messages and Library/Safari are protected by exactly the same macOS mechanism, are denied for exactly the same reason, and were not probed at all — so the report listed twelve locations, said "not full access", and stayed silent about two of the places it could not reach.

Silence is the dangerous shape here. The entire point of this tool is that the model consults it before telling Grayson something is not there. Asked "can you read my texts", it would have found no mention of Messages and no reason to doubt itself. All three now appear:

NO  mail       macOS TCC — needs Full Disk Access
NO  messages   macOS TCC — needs Full Disk Access
NO  safari     macOS TCC — needs Full Disk Access

It answered correctly, and for the wrong reason

Asked about iMessages, Cleetus said no — with tools: []. It reasoned from its own tool list rather than checking, which happened to be right and would have stayed "right" after the permission was granted. The tool's description only invited a call when "a folder looks unexpectedly empty", which a direct capability question never triggers.

It now says to call it before answering any question about what he can see, and why: permissions change and a tool list does not. Both questions are grounded now:

"can you read my imessages?"  → check_access  → "locked out by macOS privacy settings"
"can you see my Desktop?"     → check_access, list_dir → the actual folders

To close the gap

Mail, Messages and Safari need Full Disk Access granted to /opt/homebrew/bin/node — the binary cleetusd runs as — in System Settings, Privacy & Security. That is a click Grayson has to make; nothing on this side can grant it. Until then the report says so plainly rather than leaving it out. 60 tests, 35 checks.

42One repo, two writers, and an incomplete backup

Section 28 said two agents were editing this machine and nothing of one session's work had been swept into the other's commits. Half of that has since stopped being true.

There is only one repository

Checking whether ~/cleetusd had its own history produced a confident wrong answer first: git rev-parse --is-inside-work-tree said yes, and a git log run from inside it showed commits. Both were the home repo answering — /Users/grayson is itself a git repo and cleetusd is simply a directory in it. There is no nested .git. Two commands agreeing does not make an answer right if they are answering the same way for the same wrong reason; git rev-parse --show-toplevel settles it in one line.

The overlap happened

Their commit bbcdf81 touched cleetusd/src/server.mjs — a file this session had rewritten hours earlier to stop a camera hiccup killing the daemon (section 33). That fix is inside their commit. Nothing was lost and the change is now safely in history, which is the good version of this outcome. It is still exactly the thing section 28 warned about, and it happened within four hours of the warning being written.

The insurance had a hole in it

The backup taken in section 28 was git diff, which covers tracked files and nothing else. Six files this session created were untracked and therefore absent from the backup, including src/tools/mail.mjs — the entire send_email tool — and four test files.

A backup that silently omits new work is worse than none, because it is believed. It is a tarball now: 44 files, src, bin, test, tracked or not.

~/cleetus-memory/pending/cleetusd-full.tgz         44 files, everything
~/cleetus-memory/pending/cleetusd-uncommitted.patch 2,103 lines, tracked only
~/cleetus-memory/pending/cleetusv2-uncommitted.patch 1,113 lines, tracked only

What this actually asks of Grayson

Twenty-one changed files under cleetusd and twenty under cleetusv2 are uncommitted in a tree another agent commits from. None of it is lost and none of it is broken. But the longer it sits, the more of it travels in somebody else's commit — including the brief and health changes deliberately held back for him to decide on. 60 tests, 35 checks.

43The doctor had no memory

Thirty-five checks, and nothing ran them. Every report described the present and forgot it, so the one question that matters after a fault — how long has this been like this? — had no answer anywhere on the machine.

That is why the Plaid flapping took six hours to characterise. It was only ever visible because somebody sat re-running the same command by hand and noticed a pattern: ready at 19:50, no_banks at 21:00, ready at 22:00, no_banks at 22:55. Nothing recorded any of that. Had the machine been left alone, the next person would have seen one reading and had no way to tell an outage from a blink.

One line per run

2026-08-13T09:50:26Z  33/35 ok  FAIL: every-launch-agent's-program-exists agent-briefs-match-the-deployed-site
2026-08-13T09:51:12Z  33/35 ok  FAIL: every-launch-agent's-program-exists agent-briefs-match-the-deployed-site

Written by com.cleetus.health every fifteen minutes to ~/cleetus-memory/health.log, greppable by check name, trimmed at four thousand lines — about six weeks of history. Duration becomes a question you read rather than watch:

grep agent-briefs ~/cleetus-memory/health.log | head -1   # when it started
tail -20 ~/cleetus-memory/health.log                      # what is happening now

What it deliberately does not do

It writes a log. It does not restart anything, does not open a PR, and does not notify: push has been down since July, and inventing a second delivery channel at three in the morning is not this job. The deck already reads the same checks live. This exists so that when something breaks unattended there is a record of when, which is the part that was missing.

Not counted among the dead

It is an interval job, so "not running" is its normal state between firings — the exact property that made ten dead agents invisible for three months. It is therefore kept out of the always-on list the doctor watches, and covered instead by the check that its program file exists. The dead count stayed at ten, which is how you know the distinction held. 18 plists, all parsing. 60 tests.

44How long, not just what

The health log from section 43 was being written and read by nothing. A record nobody consults is a slower version of no record.

/doctor now walks that log backwards for each failing check and reports when the current streak began. Backwards through the streak, not a grep for the first occurrence ever: a check that failed yesterday, recovered, and failed again an hour ago has been failing for an hour, and any other answer is a lie about the present.

every launch agent's program exists    since 2026-08-13T09:50:26Z
agent briefs match the deployed site   since 2026-08-13T09:50:26Z

The deck shows it as an age, because "32m" reads faster than a timestamp when scanning a panel, with the full ISO time in the tooltip alongside the fix.

It rendered correctly and was invisible

First attempt appended the duration to the end of each line. The panel rows are white-space: nowrap with text-overflow: ellipsis, so the end of the line is precisely what gets clipped:

× services: every launch agent's prog…

The value was in the DOM, correct, and cut off. Information placed where it gets truncated has not been delivered. Moving it in front of the check name puts it in the part that always survives:

× 32m services: every launch agent's …
× 32m cleetusd: agent briefs match th…

Caught by screenshotting the panel rather than trusting that the API returned the field. The API had returned it correctly the whole time.

Also visible on the deck now, from section 41: MAIL, MESSAGES and SAFARI all reading DENIED in the reach panel, where previously only Mail appeared. 60 tests, 35 checks.

45Cleaning up after the testing

"Recent work" is Grayson's view of what Cleetus has been doing for him. It had filled up with questions this session asked itself.

Nine of them: turning the desk light on and off, opening Amazon twice, an invented Georgia tax credit, an email to test@example.com, "can you see my Desktop?". Every one was a real request the machine really answered — and none of them was his. They pushed his own questions off the panel entirely.

The files are kept. They are a true record of what happened, they are what the improve loop reads, and deleting history to tidy a display is the wrong trade. Each carries probe: true in its frontmatter and recentRuns() skips them — reading four times the limit first, since filtering after the slice would have made the panel shorter rather than cleaner.

Identified by exact question text, not by timestamp. The obvious approach — "anything modified in the last few hours" — would have been wrong: re-grading runs earlier tonight rewrote their mtimes, so half the session's own edits looked like fresh activity. Two ambiguous runs were left visible rather than risk hiding something he asked.

A test that passed by never running

The three assertions for this went on the end of plumbing.test.mjs — which ends in process.exit(). Node reported the file green. Nothing in the block had executed. It showed up as a suspiciously quiet "pass 1, fail 0" with none of the three check lines printed, and the count only moved from 25 to 28 once the block was placed above the exit. A test file that exits is a wall; anything appended after it is decoration.

46The summary had gone stale, which is the one thing it cannot do

Section 01 opens by claiming every line in it was checked against live behaviour. It was — at 22:00 last night. By morning it was quoting 15 tools, 24 checks and 12 access locations, none of which were still true.

That matters more than a wrong number elsewhere in the document. Section 01 is the page someone reads first and the one they trust to decide what is worth their attention. A summary that is only accurate on the day it was written is exactly the failure this whole document is about, committed by the document itself.

ClaimedActually
15 tools19 — browse gone, four web_* and send_email added
20 agents21 — the other session added studio
24 doctor checks35, running themselves every 15 minutes
12 access locations13, three of them denied rather than one
trackpad "53–83 fps"15.0 distinct/s of 16.6 delivered — the old figure counted the metric section 24 proved misleading

Rewritten against live readings taken minutes before, and the table now names what is broken as well as what works: three TCC denials, ten dead jobs, stale briefs on the deployed site, push reaching nobody. A status table that only lists successes is a brochure.

Checked by comparison, not by reading

The tool list in section 03 was updated to "19 tools" and still named browse, which has not existed since section 17 — the count was right and the contents were wrong. Rather than re-read it, the list was extracted from the page and diffed against the live registry: in the doc but not the registry: none. In the registry but not the doc: none. Two sets either match or they do not, and that answer does not depend on how carefully anyone read.

47The document checks itself now

Section 46 found this handoff quoting numbers that had drifted overnight. Nothing was checking it — which makes it the same kind of silent failure as everything else in here, in the document about silent failures.

Only the mechanically checkable part is asserted, and only one thing: the tool list this document prints, against the registry that actually exists. Prose stays a human problem. Two sets either match or they do not:

ok   handoff lists the tools that exist   19 named, all real

Verified in both directions — adding a ghost_tool to the registry turns it red with ghost_tool (unlisted), and removing it turns it green again. After four checks this session that could not fail, a negative control is not optional.

A fixed window, for the third time

The first version read 1,200 characters from the start of the tool list and flagged shell, cat, ls and grep as tools that no longer exist. They are the aliases, named in the sentence immediately after the list. The same mistake as the catch-block scanner in section 33 and the check-block scanner in section 38: a window sized in characters reads whatever happens to be next. It is bounded by the sentence that ends the list now, which is a thing that means something rather than a number that happened to be big enough.

The check skips rather than fails when the handoff cannot be read: macOS revoked this process's Desktop access for three hours last night, and a check that goes red because it could not look is noise. 36 checks, 60 tests.

48The history could not answer its own question

The health log exists because Plaid flapped for six hours and nothing recorded it. Two entries later it had recorded integrations-healthy failing — and not which integration.

That is the exact question it was built to answer. Eight integrations sit behind that one check, and a line saying one of them was down is a line saying nothing useful about which. The check name went in; the detail, where the finding actually lives, did not.

before   FAIL: integrations-healthy
after    FAIL: integrations-healthy[plaid]

Only checks whose detail is the finding get the suffix, so lines stay scannable. Now the question has a one-line answer:

grep 'integrations-healthy\[plaid\]' ~/cleetus-memory/health.log | wc -l

Tonight's method, written down where it survives this project

Five ways a check passed without proving anything, each hit at least once in one night, are now a memory rather than a story in this file:

  • A check that has never failed has not been tested — break it on purpose first.
  • An invented example confirms the code, not reality — take examples verbatim from real output.
  • A fixed-size window reads whatever is next — bound scans by a delimiter that means something.
  • Code after process.exit() never runs — if the count does not move, it did not run.
  • Verify the thing you care about, not a component of it — screenshot the panel, ask the device.

Every one produced a confident, wrong report that would have been believed. That is the whole reason they are worth keeping: none of them looked like a mistake at the time. 36 checks, 60 tests.

49The history paid for itself on the first read

Characterising the Plaid flapping by hand took six hours of re-running one command. Reading it out of the log took one query, and gave a sharper answer than the watching did.

BROKE      10:51:45      RECOVERED  11:06:55
BROKE      11:52:19      RECOVERED  12:07:30

Both dips start at roughly :51 past the hour and both are gone by the next poll. That is not a service having a bad day — it is something running on a schedule. Yesterday's hand-taken readings agree: down near 21:00, back by 22:00, down again near 22:55.

Note what the resolution actually permits: the poller runs every fifteen minutes, so "cleared within 15 minutes" is the shortest outage it can measure. The real dip may be far shorter. A measurement's floor is part of the measurement.

Ruling out the obvious suspect first — my own monitoring

A new poller had just been added that calls /api/health, which calls Plaid, four times an hour. That is exactly the sort of thing that causes the failure it then reports, so it had to be eliminated before anything else was considered.

It is not the cause. The health agent's first run was 09:50 today; the same hourly pattern was recorded by hand from 21:00 yesterday, eleven hours earlier. And four evenly spaced calls an hour cannot produce a dip at one specific minute past each hour.

Where it is not

Nothing on this Mac fires hourly: the only 3600s job is vault-sync, which is one of the ten dead. The repo has one GitHub workflow and it is the deploy. So the schedule belongs to something outside — Plaid's own item refresh, or the institution behind it — and settling that needs the Plaid dashboard, which is Grayson's to open.

What changed

"Plaid is sometimes down" became "Plaid drops at ~:51 past the hour and is back within fifteen minutes, has done since at least yesterday evening, and it is not us." That is the difference between a symptom and something you can take to a support ticket — and it cost one grep instead of an evening.

50The drift was mine

The prediction held: a third dip at 12:52:54, where section 49 said it would be, and this time the log named integrations-healthy[plaid] instead of leaving it to inference. Then the measurement fell apart under one check.

dip starts   10:51:45   11:52:19   12:52:54
gaps                  60m 34s    60m 35s

Two gaps agreeing to within a second, both a clean 35 seconds longer than an hour. A cron pinned to a clock minute repeats at exactly 3600s, so this looked like proof of an interval timer that re-arms after its work finishes — a real and useful distinction between two very different causes.

Except the poller drifts by the same amount

poll gaps (target 900s):  911 909 909 903 910 910 911 904 911 910 904 908
mean 908.4s  →  +34s per hour

A dip is recorded at the first poll that catches it, so the measurement inherits the sampler's phase. The sampler slides +34s an hour; the apparent dip slides +35s an hour. The entire finding was the instrument. At fifteen-minute resolution a clock-aligned cron and a self-re-arming timer are indistinguishable, and the confident version of that sentence was one command away from being written down as fact.

Measuring it properly

A 55-minute probe now samples /api/plaid/accounts every 60 seconds across the next predicted window, writing to ~/cleetus-memory/plaid-probe.log. A floor fifteen times finer makes the dip's true start minute readable rather than inferred, and it will say plainly whether the event lands on a clock minute or drifts.

What survives from section 49 is what was actually observed: three dips, roughly an hour apart, each cleared by the following poll, none of them caused by this machine. What does not survive is the mechanism. Same lesson as the camera counting delivered frames rather than distinct ones — a number that moves is not automatically a number about the thing being studied.

51Better sampling killed the theory, and implicated the sampler

One minute of resolution instead of fifteen, and both halves of the previous answer went away: the timing was wrong, and the measurement may have been causing what it measured.

The hourly pattern was aliasing

predicted next dip   13:53:29Z   (from three hourly-looking observations)
actual onset         13:27:45Z   26 minutes early
gap from last dip    34.9 min    not ~60

Three dips spaced fifteen-minute-samples apart looked hourly. At one-minute resolution the real gap is about thirty-five minutes. A cycle shorter than twice the sampling interval cannot be read correctly by that sampling — it folds into a slower-looking one, which is exactly what happened, and the drift in section 50 dressed it up as precision.

And the probe is a suspect

The doctor was calling that endpoint 4 times an hour. The probe called it 60 times an hour. The dip began 16 minutes after the probe started, arrived 26 minutes early, and ran longer than any previously recorded.

That may be coincidence — dips were recorded at 10:51, 11:52 and 12:52, before the probe existed. It may not be. This is Grayson's live bank connection, not a test rig, and the question "is my instrument breaking his data" is not one to keep sampling through. The probe was stopped mid-run rather than left to finish.

What is actually known

  • The dips are real, predate any monitoring here, and clear on their own.
