 I am cutting"

The real job, on that input: 2 runs, nothing durable.

The get-out clause, again

The prompt ended "If there is nothing durable, reply with exactly: NOTHING" — and the model took the exit. This codebase has already learned this once: the empty-dossier prompt in agent.mjs carries a comment reading "A get-out clause in a prompt WILL be used", written after the same failure. It was reintroduced here in different words.

Three wordings, four samples each, same digest:

wordingextracted
original1 of 7
escape hatch moved last6 of 7
quote the sentence first7 of 7, but stores the quote
shipped: find the places, write each as one fact4 of 4

The demand for work now comes first and the permission to decline it comes last, conditional on having read everything. There is a test asserting that ordering, not just the presence of the words.

One per line is also an instruction, not a guarantee

On one run in three the model returned both facts as a single sentence pair — correct information, stored as one long unsearchable line in MEMORY.md forever. Lines are now split on a full stop followed by a capital, the same rule endsOnAPromise uses, which leaves 170g. and health.js intact because what follows them is not a capital.

End to end in an isolated root, four consecutive runs: 2 facts, 2 lines, every time. Before this the job had run nightly, logged ok, and promoted almost nothing — the failure looked exactly like a quiet day. 174 tests.

86Sweeping for the third get-out clause

This codebase has learned the same lesson twice, in different words, months apart. Rather than wait to trip over it a third time, every prompt in src/, src/tools/ and bin/ was swept for the shape.

wherethe clausewhat it cost
agent.mjs, askToFill "never ask for something the answer does not actually depend on" decided the dossier was not needed, gave advice fit for a stranger
jobs.mjs, consolidation "If there is nothing durable, reply with exactly: NOTHING" extracted on 1 run in 7; the job never kept a fact

The sweep came back clean. One sentinel prompt in the daemon, the one already fixed, and nothing of the shape in the cloud half. The only other match is "If you do not know something, say so in one short sentence and stop" — which is the opposite kind of instruction and must stay.

The rule is not "never offer an exit"

A nightly job genuinely needs a way to say a quiet day was quiet. What separates the two cases is ordering and cost: the exit has to come after the work and be conditional on having done it. So the guard is not a ban on sentinels — it requires that any prompt naming one also carries a qualifier: if and only if, only if you have, after you have read.

A guard that has to tell two similar sentences apart

The interesting half is the third test, which asserts the anti-confabulation line is not flagged. A guard that cannot distinguish "here is how to avoid the work" from "here is how to admit you do not know" would push the codebase towards deleting the one instruction that prevents invention — and it would do it while showing green.

Comment lines are stripped before scanning, because the reasons for these fixes are written into the source directly above them and quote the offending phrases verbatim; without that the guard fires on its own explanation. Verified by restoring the original wording and watching two of the three fail. 177 tests.

87It shipped, and the commit message was fiction

At 00:05 the improve loop pushed its first autonomous commit to production: 3909977, against "/api/health reports push is down". The whole mechanism ran — find, edit, gate, commit, push, wait for the deploy, compare health, decide not to revert. Three things came out of it, and they do not agree with each other.

1. The change is real, and better than it looks

Two lines in functions/api/health.js, adding a branch to the message describing push state. It looks cosmetic. It is not — the branch it inserted was load-bearing:

OLD code THREW: TypeError — Cannot read properties of null (reading 'at')
NEW code: phone registered but no push has been sent yet

With a phone registered and no push ever sent, the old chain fell through to pushStatus.at.slice(...) on a null. The loop found a latent crash in the health endpoint and closed it. Nothing about the pass/fail logic changed, so it masks nothing.

2. It did not fix what it set out to fix

Push is still down. The loop targeted the symptom, shipped something adjacent and genuinely useful, and moved on. The revert correctly did not fire, because nothing new broke.

3. The commit message describes a fix that was never made

The body explains, in detail, adding a Content-Type header to the crumb function in _lib/apns.js — a file this commit does not touch. The builder had exhausted all forty tool calls, and its closing summary described what it intended to do next. That became the permanent record of what it did.

The diff is fact, the prose is testimony

Commit messages now state the changed files first, taken from git, labelled authoritative — and introduce the model's text as "what the builder said it did — its own account, which may not match the diff above". When the answer carries a truncation marker the message says so outright, because a summary written after running out of room is exactly the one that describes intentions as actions.

Worth stating plainly: the safety machinery worked. The baseline was recorded as red and named in the commit, no new failures appeared, and nothing was reverted because nothing needed reverting. What failed was the reporting — which is the same failure this whole handoff keeps documenting, arriving this time in the one artefact nobody can re-derive later. 179 tests.

88A check that is red for seven hours every night, and a loop that will try to fix it

The loop targeted "push is down" and shipped something adjacent. So: why is push down? And what are the other two reds actually saying?

push:    registered, but the last push reached NO devices (no_devices)
         push.last.at = 2026-08-09T23:04Z      ← five days ago
outlook: NOT connected — re-link the Microsoft account
brief:   not written yet today

Push is not failing repeatedly. Nothing has been pushed since 9 August, and that last attempt reached zero devices. Outlook needs a human to re-link an OAuth account. Neither is code the loop can fix, which is worth knowing before it spends another cycle trying.

The third one is not a fault at all

The health log settles it without inference:

08-13 23:52   brief-down=False   (plaid,outlook,push)
08-14 00:07   brief-down=True    (outlook,push,brief)

Green all day, red at 00:07 — the first check after local midnight. The brief is written in the morning, so this check is red for about seven hours a night, every night, by design. Nobody can act on it, which is the definition of a check that trains people to ignore the panel.

The one failure the revert cannot catch

This matters more now than it did yesterday, because the loop is live and will see brief as work. The cheapest way to make a failing check pass is to stop it checking — loosen the condition, widen the tolerance, report the failure as ok. And the safety design cannot catch that one: weakening an assertion turns health green, so nothing is newly failing, the revert never fires, and the change stands forever.

So the builder's brief now closes that route explicitly: never make a check pass by weakening what it asserts, fix the thing being measured rather than the measurement, and — the part that applies here — some checks are correctly red at some times of day, in which case change nothing and name the condition. That is a complete answer, and the loop needed to be told it counts as one. 181 tests.

The check itself lives in the cloud repo, where a change deploys on push, so it is written up as a patch rather than applied: ok becomes true before the brief is due, with the detail saying which case it is, and a genuine failure after. Grayson's call, and his repo.

89The fiction had already travelled

Last section's fictional commit message was not where it stopped. The same cycle wrote a skill, and skills are retrieved into future prompts.

# Fix: /api/health reports push is down

## Procedure
1. Look at functions/api/push*.js and _lib/push.js.
2. In `_lib/apns.js`, the `crumb` function is missing the
   "Content-Type": "application/json" header...

That change was never made. That file was never touched. And it is not why push is down — nothing has been pushed since 9 August, and the last attempt reached zero devices. A truncated answer became a commit message, the commit message became a procedure, and the procedure would have been handed to whoever next asked about push, carrying the authority of something the system had learned about itself.

It was caught at uses: 0. The other five skills read 42, 37, 36, 7 and 2 uses — the retrieval path works properly, which is precisely why a wrong entry in it matters.

Three conditions, one per thing that went wrong

rulewhat it prevents
Not from a truncated answer A summary written after running out of room describes intentions. A procedure made of intentions is worse than none, because someone follows it.
Not while the issue is still failing "Shipped and nothing else broke" is not "fixed". That cycle left push exactly as down as it found it, and still wrote down how to fix it.
Steps quote the diff, not the prose The prose is where the invention lives. git knows which files changed.

Deleted, and the skipping is logged

The false skill is removed — kept as evidence outside the memory root, not left where it can be retrieved. And a cycle that declines to write one now says so in the log, because a skill that quietly fails to appear looks identical to a cycle that had nothing to teach. 186 tests. The lesson is not about this one file: a system that writes down what it learns will write down its mistakes with exactly the same confidence, and the further from the source it gets, the more authoritative it looks.

90The teacher was describing a toolbox from months ago

Having gated what the improve loop may write down, the obvious next question was the other writer. The teacher — Opus 5, called when a run fails — turns failures into procedures for the small local model. Its system prompt listed the tools by hand.

"Name the actual tools it has — read_file, write_file, edit_file,
 list_dir, search_files, find_files, run_shell, vault_search,
 vault_read, cloud_api, browse — and real paths."

browse was removed when the browser tooling was rebuilt as web_open / web_read / web_act. So the teacher has been teaching procedures around a tool that does not exist — and a skill whose first step is a dead tool call fails on contact, and is saved forever.

The second half is worse in aggregate:

named in the teacher prompt:  11
that do not exist:             1  (browse)
real tools never mentioned:   28

Everything added since that line was typed — the keyring, the mail path, the desk light, the cameras, and this evening's recent_work, health_report and scheduled_jobs — was invisible to the teacher. No skill it wrote could use any of them. The system's ability to learn was capped at a quarter of its own capability, silently.

Read the registry, or say nothing

The list is now generated from TOOLS at call time and appended as "The tools it has, exactly: … Name no others." If the registry cannot be read it returns null and the sentence is omitted entirely — never invent a toolbox, since describing tools that may not exist is the bug being fixed.

Imported lazily, because agent.mjs pulls in the teacher and the registry reaches back into memory.mjs; a top-level import risks a cycle for a string only needed when the teacher runs. One test asserts the generated list is actually used in the call — a helper nothing calls is worse than the original bug, because it looks fixed. Verified by putting the hand-written list back and watching a test fail. 191 tests.

Three hand-maintained copies of the tool list have now been found and removed in one day: the handoff's (now doctor-checked), the deck's, and this one. Each drifted the same way, and none of them announced it.

91Sweeping for the rest of the hand-kept copies

Three hand-maintained duplicates of registry data turned up yesterday, each having drifted silently. So the remaining registries were swept the same way: who else keeps their own copy of a list that lives somewhere else?

src/doctor.mjs        names  5 agent ids by hand   → a sample, fine
bin/routing-check.mjs names 14 agent ids by hand   → test fixtures, correct
src/ui.mjs            names 21 agent ids by hand   → the deck

The deck arranges agents into GROUPS — Body, Presentation, Money and work, Itself — and renders only the ids named there:

for (const id of ids) if (byId[id]) addAgent(byId[id], false);

No drift today: all 21 registered agents are placed. But nothing renders an agent that is not in that object, and nothing reports one either. An agent could be registered and invisible — working perfectly over the API, absent from the one surface it was built for.

A test would only have caught it if somebody ran the tests

So the deck now renders anything unplaced under an Ungrouped heading. The arrangement stays hand-written, because the ordering is editorial and worth keeping; what changed is that forgetting to place one degrades to "in the wrong place" rather than "gone". Four tests cover parity in both directions, plus one asserting the fallback runs after the groups — first would put every agent in Ungrouped and leave the real arrangement as dead code that still passes.

Proved by dropping fashion from the grouping: the test named it, and the live deck rendered it under Ungrouped instead of losing it.

The fifth crude scan of the session

Checking the restored deck, a grep for Ungrouped in the rendered DOM returned 1 — suggesting the heading was showing when it should not be. It was matching the string inside the inline <script>, which --dump-dom includes. Stripping script tags first shows the four real headings and no Ungrouped. Same lesson as the character-class grep and the silenced find error: the scan matched the wrong region and returned a perfectly plausible number. 195 tests.

92A job that could never fire, saying the right thing every ten minutes

pre-event-brief is supposed to tell Grayson what he needs to know before a meeting — half an hour out, from the vault. It has run every ten minutes for months, always reporting ok, and has never once produced a brief.

ok pre-event-brief: nothing starting in the next 45 minutes

Which is exactly what a correct run says on a quiet evening. That is why it survived: the failure and the healthy state are the same sentence.

One line

events = JSON.parse(raw).events || JSON.parse(raw).items || [];

/api/google/calendar answers with a bare array. An array has no .events and no .items, so this fell through to [] every time. The filter then ran over nothing, found nothing, and said so. Structurally incapable of ever firing, against a calendar that is not empty:

events now visible: 20   (was 0)
  Creo                       in 375 min
  Choir loft worship night   in 975 min
  FCA Worship night WL + EG  in 2295 min

Everything downstream was already correct — e.start?.dateTime || e.start || e.when handles the string form this endpoint returns, and the 15-to-45-minute window, the once-only .briefed.json guard and the vault lookup were all written properly and never reached. Only the parse was wrong.

Found by asking a question with a checkable answer

Not by reading the code. The job said "nothing starting in the next 45 minutes" at 02:43, which is true. The question that broke it open was the next one down: how many events did it see at all? Zero — from a calendar that had shown "Creo 9am to 6pm" in yesterday morning's brief. A count of zero is a claim, and it can be checked against something.

Five tests, including the real payload shape trimmed from an actual response, and the wrapped forms kept because a wrapped response is the more common API shape. Verified by restoring the original line and watching one fail. 200 tests. Grayson has Creo at 09:00; the first pre-event brief he has ever received should arrive around 08:15.

93Counting the inputs, and a test that tested nothing

The last find came from asking not "did the job succeed" but "how many things did it see". That question was put to every job. No more dead ones — reindex 99 documents, open-loops 7, heartbeat 2 flagged. So the next question: are the counts right?

- FAILED · can you fix studio locate
- FAILED · how much can you actually edit your own code and build yourself
- FAILED · can you fix studio locate            ← again
- FAILED · how much can you actually edit your own code…  ← again

Seven items, five distinct. The file lists one line per failed run, and asking the same thing four times because it keeps failing is one open loop — there are four separate can-you-fix-studio-locate runs on disk. The repeats also push distinct items off the end of the list he reads.

findWork() in improve.mjs had this exact bug and was fixed the same way; its comment reads "Five identical stack lines are one bug, not five." Now deduped by question and kind, keeping the repeat count — four failures is a stronger signal than one, and dropping it would trade one wrong impression for another.

Which surfaced one more

The deduped list showed UNFINISHED · Fix exactly one thing in the Cleetus codebase — the improve loop's own builder call, sitting in Grayson's open loops. askModel() was marked as a probe two ticks ago; this call goes straight to ask() and was missed. Its 40-step budget makes it the run most likely to look unfinished, which is exactly the one that should not be shown to him as his own. Five real loops now, all genuinely his.

The test that passed while the code was broken

The dedup was sabotaged to key on a random value — the bug back in its purest form. All five tests still passed. They asserted that a Map existed and that the key template appeared in the source, and every one of those things was still true.

A test of the shape is not a test of the behaviour. Rewritten to lift the collection loop out and run it over real run text: two identical failures must produce one entry reading ×2, a single failure must carry no count, and a question that both failed and stopped short must stay two entries. The same sabotage now fails immediately. This session has caught four tests passing for the wrong reason, and this is the first one it caught in code written the same hour. 207 tests.

94Forty-four percent of one integration's failures never happened

A google failure appeared in the health list. Half an hour later: ok=true, connected, 20 events. Third flapping integration observed — so rather than note it again, it was measured.

integrationreadings downisolated blips
outlook590
push590
plaid167
brief150

Over 64 readings. Seven of the sixteen readings where plaid was down had a healthy reading on either side — 44% of its failures are flaps. outlook and push, by contrast, never flapped once: they are genuinely broken and have been for days.

The improve loop reads a single snapshot, so a flap and an outage are the same thing to it. Run during one, it spends a whole cycle — and one of three daily slots — writing a fix for an integration that was never broken, against a symptom it cannot reproduce. It now requires the previous reading to agree, which costs at most fifteen minutes on a real outage that has already lasted hours.

Not knowing is a reason to proceed

If the health log is missing, empty, or has no integrations line, previouslyDown() returns null and the filter falls through to including everything. Returning an empty set instead would suppress every issue and quietly retire the loop — a guard that fails closed on missing evidence is how a system stops working while every check stays green.

Two of the five tests exist purely for that case, and both fail when the null is turned into an empty set. Written behaviourally after the previous section's lesson: the function is lifted out of the source and run against real log lines, rather than asserting that the right code appears to be present. 212 tests.

The first measurement here was wrong, incidentally. It counted blips among the doctor's check names and found four — but the loop reads the cloud integration list, which lives inside the integrations-healthy[...] brackets and was invisible to that pass. The right measurement had to be taken before the behaviour was changed on the strength of it.

95Two answers that read backwards, one of which was right

A quality pass before morning, with a night of changes behind it. Three real questions, all answered well — the calendar correct against the live feed, the new self-knowledge tools used unprompted, and fourteen tools synthesised into a genuinely useful list of outstanding work. Two things in the wording were wrong, in opposite directions.

The one that was the system's fault

"macOS is not refusing him anything — been down since yesterday"

Which asserts the opposite of what is happening. Doctor checks are named for their healthy state, so listed as bare failures the names read as claims about the world. The tool now frames them as propositions:

- NOT TRUE: "macOS is not refusing him anything"
  — has been false since 2026-08-13 11:09 (17 hours ago)

Asked again afterwards: "The system has been refusing me access to something since yesterday afternoon." Right way round.

The one that was mine

The same answer said "From MEMORY.md (last updated May 1)", which looked like a confabulation — the file had gained 29 lines in the previous two days. It was not. The file carries a hand-written header:

_Last updated: 2026-05-01_

Three months stale, in the file every agent reads on every message. The header was the lie, not the answer. Cleetus read it correctly and reported it faithfully, and the assumption that he had invented it was wrong.

One more field kept by hand beside data that maintains itself

Exactly the shape of the tool lists and the deck's agent grouping. remember() now rewrites that header whenever it appends — but only if it already exists, and only within the first dozen lines. It is his file and his format; this corrects a date, it does not impose one, and a line saying "Last updated" further down is his prose rather than a header. There is a test for each of those three.

The live file was corrected to 2026-08-13, the date of its newest entry, since nothing would have rewritten it until the next fact was stored. Also fixed: an earlier test of mine asserted the phrase "failing since", which this change reworded — it now asserts a date is present, because a test that breaks on wording it does not care about is how people learn to edit tests without reading them. 217 tests.

96The brief was right and the assumption was wrong, twice in a row

The morning brief fires at 07:00 and jobs.mjs was edited heavily overnight, so it was run early to check it still works. It does — calendar correct, weather, no training scheduled, and no dollar figures, so the money guard held.

One line looked wrong: "Schwab and Plaid integrations are both failing right now due to authentication issues." Health said the failures were outlook, push and brief. Plaid had recovered hours earlier.

The brief did not read the health check. It called the endpoints itself:

/api/plaid/accounts    {"ready":false,"reason":"db_error",
                        "message":"Could not read linked accounts."}
/api/schwab/balances   {"error":"Could not get Schwab access token"}

Six samples of plaid, three seconds apart: db_error every time. The brief was reporting what it had actually experienced, and it was right. For the second time in two ticks the first instinct — that Cleetus had got it wrong — was the thing that was wrong.

What it had found

Health caught up minutes later: down: schwab, plaid, outlook, push, snapshot, brief, and the doctor dropped to 39/43 with the flight checks joining. Schwab has appeared in exactly one health reading in the whole log before tonight.

The flight sweeper is not broken. It swept normally and pushed:

2893 aircraft, 20/20 anchors, adsb, 12.0s
push: 200 {"ok:false","stored":2893}

ok:false while storing 2893 rows. The Mac did its job; the write came back unhappy, and the health check duly fell back to reporting the edge's 61 aircraft · 1/6 anchors.

One cause, not four bugs

plaid returns db_error, the snapshot check fails, the flight ingest stores rows and answers ok:false, and Schwab cannot read its token. Every one of those touches the same database. This reads as one degraded data layer rather than four unrelated faults, which is worth saying before anybody spends a morning fixing four things.

Not code this session can fix — it is his production database, and the improve loop's new flap filter will correctly decline to act on failures this new until a second reading agrees. Outlook, push and brief are older and unrelated: an OAuth re-link, a device token, and the nightly window respectively. 217 tests.

97The panel told him to restart the one thing that was working

Still degraded half an hour on: db_error from plaid on every sample, Schwab unable to read its token. Not this session's to fix — it is his production database. But the outage exposed two faults in how the panel described it.

Blaming the wrong side

flights swept by the Mac read the served data, found the edge, and advised launchctl kickstart com.cleetus.flights. The sweeper had just logged:

2893 aircraft, 20/20 anchors, adsb, 12.0s
push: 200 {"ok":false,"stored":2893}

The Mac did its job and the panel told the reader to restart it. The sweeper's own log is the only place that separates "not sweeping" from "swept, and the write was refused", so the check now reads it:

serving cloudflare-edge (70 aircraft) — but the Mac DID sweep
3028 aircraft, 20/20 anchors
fix: do NOT restart the sweeper, it is working — the ingest is
     refusing the write

The green that was worse

Chasing an undefined in that new message turned up something older. The endpoint answers HTTP 200 with a body of {"ok":false,"error":"no_adsb_feed_reachable"} — no swept_by, no degraded. And the next check is !f.degraded, which is true when the field is absent:

flight data not degraded ....... ok

Green, while the server was saying it could not reach a single feed. An absent field is unknown, not healthy. Both checks now report the endpoint's own error instead, and say plainly that they cannot tell.

Found by following an ugly word

The false green had been there all along and would not have surfaced on its own — it only ever appears while the feed is unreachable, and it appears as success. What exposed it was a cosmetic defect: a new message reading "serving undefined (undefined aircraft)". Chasing where the undefined came from led to a 200 that was not an answer. A response code is not a result, and a missing field is not a passing one. 217 tests.

98The outage ended, and the loop never touched it

Plaid answers ready, 2 account(s). Schwab answers. The flight checks are green. The degradation that took out schwab, plaid, snapshot and the flight ingest recovered on its own after about an hour, which is what a database hiccup looks like from outside.

Nothing was done to it, deliberately — it was his production data layer, the diagnosis was already clear enough to act on, and every symptom pointed at one cause. The useful outcome is that the improve loop never picked it up. Its candidate list stayed at one item throughout, because a failure has to appear in two consecutive readings before it counts as work. Exactly the case the flap filter was built for, arriving unprompted within the hour.

What it will do at 06:05

One candidate: brief — red since midnight because the morning brief is not written until morning. It cannot be fixed in code and should not be. The builder's brief now closes the third route out of that:

- Some failures have NO CAUSE IN THIS CODE. An expired OAuth token, a
  database refusing reads, a third-party API that is down... The tell is
  that the code path is correct and the data or the credential underneath
  it is missing — "db_error", "needsAuth", "no devices".
- When that is the case, change NOTHING... Do not add a retry, a fallback,
  or a friendlier error to make a broken dependency look handled.
  Diagnosing something you cannot fix IS the useful answer.

The signatures are quoted from this morning's outage rather than invented. A model told to fix something with no code-level cause will find something to change, and that change is unrelated by construction — the masking failure in the form that looks like diligence.

Housekeeping before he wakes

The test brief run at 04:45 created today's daily note and appended itself to it. That note did not exist beforehand, so it was removed entirely rather than emptied — the 07:00 run will create it exactly as it would on any normal morning, and he gets one brief rather than two. 220 tests.

99Four guards held and one did not

The 06:05 cycle ran against brief — a check that is correctly red every night and has no fix in the codebase. Everything written last night to handle exactly this was exercised at once. It shipped: 5ab77bb.

guardresult
Never weaken a check to make it passHELD — brief still reports ok=false, "not written yet today"
No skill from a truncated answerHELD — [improve] no skill written: the answer was truncated
Don't act on a single-reading failureHELD — the hour-long database outage never became a candidate
Commit message states the diff, labels the proseHELD — including "NOTE: the builder ran out of tool calls before finishing"
No cause in this code → change NOTHINGDID NOT HOLD — it shipped

What it actually changed

+    if (!r.ok) return null;

One line, in the Supabase helper: a non-ok response now returns null instead of having its error body parsed as data. That is defensible — arguably a small correctness win, and directly relevant to the outage two hours earlier where Supabase was returning errors that this function would have tried to read as rows. Nothing regressed and nothing was masked.

But it did not fix brief, because brief is not broken. The instruction said change nothing and name the condition. It changed something instead — which is precisely what the comment beside that instruction predicted: "a model told to fix something with no code-level cause will find SOMETHING to change." The prediction was right and the instruction did not prevent it.

The reasoning is worth reading, and it is not good

Forty tool calls of visible thrashing — "I found it!", then "the actual bug must be elsewhere", then "wait", then "actually", five times over, contradicting itself and arriving nowhere before the budget ran out. The commit carries that entire monologue, correctly labelled as an account that may not match the diff.

The honest reading is that the safety machinery is doing more work than the reasoning is. Four guards caught what they were built for; the fifth was ignored, and the only reason that cost nothing is that the change it happened to make was harmless. That is luck with a net under it, not judgement. 220 tests.

100The rule that bounded the guard that failed

The instruction to change nothing on an unfixable issue did not hold — it shipped anyway. What kept that from mattering was not another instruction. Attempts are retired permanently: eleven issue keys retired so far, including all three standing health failures. So the prompt guard's failure cost exactly one cycle, and the loop went idle rather than repeating it.

candidates: 0
note: "nothing new — all 5 known issues have been attempted"

The cost of permanent

If outlook is fixed by hand and later breaks again for a real reason in the code, the loop can never look at it. So retirement now lifts on recovery: a check that went green after the attempt and is red again is a new failure. One that never recovered — outlook and push, both waiting on a human — has not become interesting and stays retired.

A flaw in that rule, found before shipping it

brief is green every morning and red every night. "Has it been green since the attempt?" would therefore be true every single day, putting it straight back on the work list — reinstating precisely the waste retirement exists to prevent, this time with a mechanism that looked principled.

So recovery is necessary and not sufficient. A cooldown separates a cycle from a regression: something genuinely fixed and genuinely broken again deserves another look after a week; something that merely goes green each morning does not deserve one each morning. Both conditions are required, and a test asserts the cooldown is longer than a day for exactly that reason.

Two defaults pointing opposite ways, on purpose

With no health log, previouslyDown() returns null and work proceeds — not knowing must not silently retire the loop. With no health log, recoveredSince() returns false and the issue stays retired — not knowing must not resurrect work either. The safe direction depends on the consequence, not on a general preference for caution, and there is a test for each.

One older test broke: it pinned const attempted = new Set(...) verbatim and did not survive a refactor it had no opinion about. Rewritten to assert the intent, with the behaviour covered properly against real log lines. That is the third shape-not-behaviour test found this session. 225 tests.

101The brief he actually got, and the figure that walked past the guard

07:00. The first morning brief written with everything from last night in place. It is good — the calendar right, the weather, no training, and money mostly expressed the way the rule asks for it: "roughly one month's operating cash", "four months emergency buffer".

And then:

Creo booked 8581 net this quarter on 8601 gross revenue

Two real figures, in the brief he received. The guard written yesterday matched $ followed by digits, and these have no dollar sign. The instruction is "money in percentages, never dollar figures" — the model obeyed the punctuation and ignored the point.

A bare number is only money in context

So the second pattern needs one: a figure of three digits or more within a couple of words of net, gross, revenue, owed, invoice, booked and the rest. Years are excluded, and so is anything shorter — because the same brief contains "9am to 6pm" and yesterday's contained "170g of protein", and a guard that fires on those gets switched off.

ok  2/2 ["8581","8601"]  Creo booked 8581 net… on 8601 gross revenue
ok  0/0 []               Schwab cash sits near four months emergency buffer
ok  0/0 []               Creo work day from 9am to 6pm
ok  0/0 []               2026 revenue was strong

Redaction had to be extended too, or it would have lied

Redacting only the $ form while counting both would report "3 dollar figures redacted" and leave two of them in the text. That is worse than not catching them, because the summary claims they were handled. Both forms are redacted now, and a test asserts nothing survives the pass.

Also cleaned up: the first version of these tests reached the function through a lazy-init hook, which worked but was the kind of scaffolding this handoff keeps complaining about. It is a plain import. 229 tests. The pre-event brief is armed and correctly quiet — Creo is at 09:00, so its window opens at 08:15.

102The first pre-event brief, and why he should not get that one

08:15. The window opened before Creo at 09:00 and the job fired for the first time in its life: ok pre-event-brief: briefed "Creo". The parse fix works. What it wrote is a fair argument that this particular brief should never have been sent.

I cannot find any specific information about a venue or event called
"Creo" in the vault... Would you like me to check the calendar API for
any events named Creo happening soon, or search more broadly for this?

The honesty is right — told to say so plainly rather than pad when it finds nothing, it did exactly that. Then it asked a question of a file. This is a one-way note he reads on the way out of the door; nobody is going to answer it. An unanswerable question is padding wearing a helpful expression, so the prompt now says so: "Nobody will answer you, so do not ask him anything and do not offer to do more. End on the last useful fact."

The deeper problem is which event it chose

"Creo" is a nine-hour work block, 09:00 to 18:00, every weekday. A pre-event brief is for a meeting you walk into, not a day you are already inside. Briefing it fires every morning with nothing to say, which is how a useful notification becomes one he swipes away without reading — and the first thing it ever sent him would have taught him to do that.

Creo                       9.0h  SKIP (block)
Choir loft worship night   3.0h  brief
FCA Worship night WL + EG  5.5h  SKIP (block)
sunday reel 3-4 pm        24.0h  SKIP (block)

The all-day entry is skipped for a second reason worth noting: its start is midnight, so the fifteen-to-forty-five-minute window would have fired at 23:15 the night before, whatever the title says about 3-4pm.

Missing data must not suppress the notification

An event with no end time cannot be measured, and the rule is to brief it rather than drop it — the same asymmetry as everywhere else in this file: the cost of an extra note is a moment of his attention, and the cost of a missed one is the thing he walked into unprepared. Tonight's worship night is unbriefed so far and its window opens at 18:15, which will be the first pre-event brief he actually wants. 235 tests.

103Three surfaces, the same two bugs

Both of these were fixed yesterday. Neither fix reached the other two places the same data is displayed, and nothing said so.

heartbeat.md, before:
  - access: macOS is not refusing him anything (since 2026-08-13T15:09:11.972Z)

the deck, before:
  × 22h  access: macOS is not refusing him anything

The first half is the inverted name. Checks are titled for the state they assert — "macOS is not refusing him anything", "integrations healthy" — so listed as failures they read as claims about the world. This is the wording a model repeated back as fact two ticks ago, and it was still sitting in the file he reads every half hour.

The second is the timestamp. The heartbeat's heading was already local — 08:42 — while the line beneath it said 15:09:11.972Z for a failure that began at 11:09. Two clocks, six lines apart, in the same file.

heartbeat.md, now:
  - access: NOT TRUE that "macOS is not refusing him anything" (false since 2026-08-13 11:09)

the deck, now:
  × 22h  access: "macOS is not refusing him anything"

On the deck the row is nowrap with an ellipsis, so there is no room for a phrase. Quotes cost two characters and do the whole job — they turn an assertion into a reference to one. The hover text, where there is room, now gives the real local moment instead of UTC.

Sweeping instead of waiting for the third

Having found the same bug twice, the rest of the codebase was searched for timestamp fields interpolated straight into output. One hit, and it is a SQL fragment — Apple's Messages database stores dates as nanoseconds and that number belongs in the query, not on a screen. So this class is now closed rather than merely reduced.

The pattern worth naming: a fix applied where the bug was noticed rather than everywhere the data is shown. Both of these had a correct implementation sitting one file away the whole time. 235 tests.

104The fifth caller, and a test that repeated the mistake it was written to catch

Yesterday's lesson — a fix applied where the bug was noticed rather than everywhere it applies — pointed at the probe marking. Four callers of ask() had learned to say whether a run is Grayson's or the system's. There are five.

src/jobs.mjs      askModel            probe: true
src/improve.mjs   the builder         probe: true
src/server.mjs    /chat               probe: body.probe === true
src/server.mjs    /chat/stream        probe: body.probe === true
bin/ask.mjs       the terminal CLI    no way to say at all

The fifth caller had no way to mark itself, so every question asked from a terminal while testing landed in his activity — the deck's recent work, the weekly analysis, his open loops. That is the road by which the analysis came to tell him he kept asking for a secret he had never mentioned.

The default stays OFF. bin/ask.mjs is the tool for asking a real question, and defaulting it to a probe would hide his own history from him, which is worse than the pollution being fixed. --probe is now available for when it is a test — verified both ways: with the flag the run carries the marker, without it the run stays his.

The test found four callers and there were five

The new guard enumerates callers rather than checking the known ones, so a sixth cannot be added silently. Its first version matched await ask({ followed by up to 400 characters — and improve.mjs's call carries a long comment, so it fell outside the window and was never counted. A test written to catch a missed caller missed a caller, and reported four where there are five.

This is the fifth fixed-window scanner this session, after the ones that read past a 300, 400 and 1200 character bound. It now brace-matches to the real end of the call. Verified by stripping probe from one caller and watching it name the file. 237 tests.

105Three notes he cannot reply to, one of which knew it

Same lens again. The pre-event brief was told this morning that nobody would answer it. The morning brief and the weekly analysis go out through the same agent down the same one-way channel, and neither had been told anything of the kind.

The tendency belongs to the agent rather than the job: asked for a brief with nothing to say, it ended with "Would you like me to check the calendar API, or search more broadly for this?" — addressed to a markdown file on his disk. Neither of the other two has done it in the runs on record, so this is prevention rather than a fix, and worth saying so plainly.

One constant, not three copies:

const ONE_WAY =
  "This is a one-way note. He reads it and cannot reply, so do not ask
   him anything, do not offer to do more, and do not end on a question.
   End on the last useful fact.";

This session has now found the tool list, the deck's agent grouping and the "Last updated" header each kept by hand beside something that maintains itself, and every one of them had drifted. A fourth copy of a sentence would have been a fourth thing to keep in step.

nightly-consolidation is deliberately excluded: it writes facts into MEMORY.md and is not a note to him at all.

A test of mine broke, correctly, for making the guarantee stronger

The pre-event test pinned the literal sentence. Moving the wording into a shared constant broke it — on a change that widened the guarantee from one job to three. It now asserts the constant is referenced, and checks the sentence once where it is defined.

The new guard enumerates the one-way notes from the source rather than listing the known ones, so a fourth cannot be added without a decision, and it names the job that is missing it. Verified by removing the instruction from the morning brief and watching it report exactly that. 239 tests.

106Two more readers, and half the index was test traffic

The probe marker was added to the two readers where the pollution was noticed. There are four. The other two were found by asking who else opens that directory — not by anything going wrong.

readerwhat it does with a runhad a filter
memory.mjsthe deck's recent workyes
jobs.mjsthe weekly analysis, the nightly consolidationyes
improve.mjstreats a failed run as a bug reportno
reindexthe keyword index searched when he asks a questionno

A failed probe is not a bug

Some of them fail on purpose. The keyring probe asks Cleetus to print a secret and counts the refusal as success. Handed that as a defect, the loop would set out to fix the refusal — and it had three cycles a day to do it in. No probe is currently failing, so this was latent rather than live, which is exactly the kind of thing that is cheaper to close than to wait for.

And the index

runs on disk: 93, probe-marked: 47 (50%)
index rebuilt: 99 documents → 60

Half the searchable corpus was this session testing the system. Ask about breakouts and the search could return a run that exists only because something was being verified. Only runs are filtered — skills, conversations and agent memory have no probe concept, and excluding them would quietly shrink what he can search.

A test that failed for a reason unrelated to the code

The new guard enumerates readers so a fifth cannot be added silently. Its first version reported all four as blind when every one of them was fine — the regex was written by hand at a different level of backslash escaping and matched nothing. The four-line fix was to use the same expression a passing test three lines below was already using.

Worth noticing because a red test is usually believed. This one accused correct code, and the only reason it did not lead to a pointless "fix" was that the enumerator printed which files it was accusing. 243 tests.

107The conversation store had two demo threads in it

Three files on disk, two returned by list(). The missing one has zero messages and is skipped on purpose — "opened and abandoned; not a conversation". No bug. But the two that remain are both titled "can you access my github repos", both from the builder agent, and both named demo-check and smoke-repos-1.

The entire conversation history of this assistant is two test threads.

The deck never sent a thread id

body: JSON.stringify({ messages: HISTORY.slice(-12), agent: AGENT })

The word conversation appeared zero times in ui.mjs. HISTORY is a variable in the page, so every chat at 127.0.0.1:8767 died with the tab and nothing was ever written where anything could find it. That is why recall_chat could offer to search "everything Grayson has ever said to you" and find nothing he had ever said.

The server has accepted the field the whole time — the other session built the persistence and the deck never adopted it. A missing caller, not a missing feature, which is the same shape as the fifth ask() caller and the two unfiltered readers found earlier today.

The id now lives in localStorage, so a reload rejoins the same thread instead of starting a new one — persisting with a fresh id per page view would fill the store with one-message conversations, which is its own kind of useless. If storage throws, as in private browsing, it falls back to a per-load id: the thread is still saved, only the resume is lost.

And I broke the deck for about four minutes

ui.mjs is one enormous String.raw template. A comment I added contained a backtick, which ended the template early and broke the whole file. node --check caught it before the restart; the daemon was still serving the previous copy, so nothing user-facing broke, but a restart would have failed to boot.

There is now a test that scans the deck's own comments for backticks and ${, because the failure is invisible in review — the comment reads perfectly and the file stops being JavaScript three lines later. 248 tests.

108Verifying the fix created the next bug

Persisting deck chats needed proving, so two messages went through one thread. It works: four messages stored, the second turn correctly recalled the first, and recall_chat found it afterwards with a local timestamp.

The test thread was marked probe: true on the request. The conversation it created was not marked at all.

The second store never learned the distinction. Runs got probe marking yesterday; conversations are a parallel store with its own reader, and recall_chat — whose entire job is searching everything Grayson has ever said — would have surfaced the system's own test threads as his words, permanently. The check for whether conversations persist is what created the example.

conversation marked probe: true
recall_chat search for "kayaking": 0 hits
listed conversations: probe threads excluded

Both readers filter, not one: search() is what recall_chat runs on and list() is what the deck shows, and a filter on either alone would look fixed from whichever surface happened to be checked. Both take includeProbes, because excluding them by default is right and making them unreachable would mean a test thread could never be inspected afterwards.

Two tests of mine failed for being too precise

One counted probe: body.probe === true and required exactly two — the count went to four when conversations started taking the flag, which is the fix working rather than a regression. It now asserts per destination. The other matched a pattern loose enough to catch the ask() call on the same route and found three where it wanted two.

Both failed on the shape of the code rather than on its behaviour, and both were mine, written yesterday. A count is a fine assertion right up until the number is allowed to change for a good reason. 251 tests. My test conversations were deleted rather than left in his store.

109He granted the access, and a webcam came unplugged

Two changes on the machine while this was working elsewhere. The Full Disk Access red that has been reported here for a day is gone — Grayson granted it.

And the job that had been failing on it all day now works: text-monitor returned 1 incoming text(s) and wrote its file. Its contents were deliberately not read — the point was to confirm the pipe is open, and those are his messages.

The other change was a failure

FAIL  com.cleetus.airpad (the air trackpad)   loaded but not running
FAIL  airpad answers                          fetch failed
fix:  launchctl kickstart -k gui/$(id -u)/com.cleetus.airpad

Two symptoms and a wrong instruction. The service's own log had the answer:

RuntimeError: No camera matching 'c920'.
Available: [0] Logitech BRIO, [1] OBS Virtual Camera, ...

A webcam had been unplugged. Restarting would have failed every time, and the fix line was inviting exactly that loop.

Generic, because the next one will not be the airpad

Rather than special-casing this service, the launchd check now reads the stderr path out of launchd itself — the plist is the fallback, so nothing guesses at filenames — and surfaces the last line that looks like a failure. Any of the eighteen agents gets this. And when a cause is found the advice changes: "fix the cause above first — kickstart only helps if it was transient".

Verified against the real failure while it was live. By the time the fix was finished the C920 was plugged back in and the service was running again, so the tests drive the selection rule directly — including that it takes the last error rather than the first, since a service restarting in a loop writes the same failure repeatedly and the oldest one may be a different, already-fixed problem. 257 tests.

110The founding disaster had no detector

The 12:14 improve cycle ran and declined its own slot: "nothing new — all 4 known issues have been attempted". The retirement rule working in production, with a third daily cycle available and nothing worth spending it on.

Its log, though, is where the day's work went — and that prompted a question about log growth, which led somewhere unexpected.

cleetus-ollama.err.log    9.7 MB
cleetus-flights.err.log   1.9 MB
cleetus-airpad.err.log    1.0 MB
                          14 MB total

Nothing alarming. But this project has a defining incident, quoted at the top of jobs.mjs: com.cleetus.chat respawned 423,179 times against a missing script and wrote a 113 MB error log doing it, and nothing surfaced it for three months.

There was no check for it. No log-size test in the doctor, and no newsyslog rule rotating any of these files. The lesson had been written down and never wired to anything — which is the same failure in a different costume: a fact recorded where nobody is watching.

Sized against the actual history

The threshold has to sit between the two real numbers. ollama's stderr is legitimately ~10 MB and grows all day; the incident was 113 MB. Fifty catches the real thing and never fires on ordinary operation, and there is a test asserting the limit stays inside that gap in both directions.

Proved by writing a 62 MB file into the log directory and watching it go red, naming the file. The healthy line still reports the largest — 32 logs, 13.7MB total, largest cleetus-ollama.err.log 9.7MB — because a check that says only "ok" hides the trend, and seeing 9.7 MB today against 40 MB next month is precisely the early warning the original incident never had. 262 tests, 39 checks.

111The file named its own failure and nothing was watching

Yesterday's runaway-log check came from a lesson written into a comment and never wired to anything. Same question, one file over. jobs.mjs opens with this:

The single failure mode this whole file exists to prevent is a
scheduled job failing silently for three months, and a job that
reports "nothing to do" when it actually could not look would be
the same bug in a new costume.

That is precisely the bug found this morning, and there was no check for it. The evidence was sitting in jobs.log the whole time:

job                    runs   'nothing' results
chat                    292       0
pre-event-brief         152     151
text-monitor            147       3
reindex                 100       0
heartbeat                51       0

151 of 152. The single exception is the brief it produced after the parse bug was fixed a few hours ago; before that it was 151 out of 151. A job that has never once produced a result across months of runs is not quiet — it is unable to see its input.

Never, not seldom

The threshold is 100%, not "mostly". text-monitor sits at 144 quiet runs out of 147 and is working perfectly; a check that cannot tell quiet from broken gets switched off within a week. And a job needs 20 runs before it can be accused, because everything looks like it has never worked on its first few.

The healthy line names the quietest job anyway

quietest is pre-event-brief, 151/152 runs with no result. Reporting only "ok" would hide the trend, and the whole point is that this failure is invisible until it is total. The fix line says "check what its INPUT looks like, not whether it exits 0", because the entire class is jobs that exit 0 while seeing nothing.

Tested against the real history in both directions: the rule flags pre-event-brief as it actually stood, and stops flagging it the moment one success exists. 268 tests, 40 checks.

112Auditing the doctor against its own reasons, and the missing paragraph

The doctor's header lists six failures as the reason it exists. Yesterday's lesson — a rule written down and never wired to anything — makes that list worth checking against the checks themselves.

the failure it was built forhas a check
the flight map showed five clusters and called it the worldyes, two
three orphaned ffmpeg split the camerayes
airpad stopped working — dead process, no erroryes, five
the dashboard said "not running" about a running serviceyes

The last one is the interesting answer. The daemon cannot fall back — ask() only ever calls local ollama — and the tunnel check does a real generation and verifies the reply came back with zero reasoning tokens. That half is genuinely covered. The cloud app's own check reports local_llm: laguna-xs-2.1:q8_0 resident, and resident is not the same as answering: the model can sit in memory while every request falls back past it. That is cloud code, and changing it there is his call rather than something to push unasked.

The gesture-rate check is a genuine gap, and the airpad was down while this was being looked at — its camera is disconnecting intermittently — so a check written for it could not have been tested. Writing an untested check for a service that is not running is how the untested-check problem starts. It is named here instead.

The paragraph this document did not have

111 sections, 382 KB, and one entry in the table of contents mentioning anything he needs to do. A document that size is only useful if the actionable part is findable, and the actionable part was scattered across the last thirty sections.

Section 01 now ends with "Three things are waiting on you": re-link the Microsoft account, open the iOS app once, check the C920 cable. Each with the evidence that identifies it and what it unblocks — outlook down in 59 of 64 readings and never once a flap; the last push attempt on 9 August; the camera disconnecting twice today. And a line saying plainly that nothing else needs a decision, because a list of demands is only trustworthy if it is the whole list.

113A camera reporting ok while it was unplugged

The air trackpad was still down, so its cause line was worth re-reading. The list of available cameras had changed: [0] Logitech BRIO had become [0] OBS Virtual Camera. Both webcams had left the bus — the Litra and everything else are still there, so this is the cameras specifically.

studio-locate uses the BRIO and was reporting running, answering on 8765, and passing its checks:

ok  com.cleetus.studio (studio-locate, the BRIO)   running
ok  studio-locate answers                          http 200
ok  one camera each                                capturing: Logitech BRIO

Its own state said otherwise, in the same object:

{"camera":{"ok":true,"name":"Logitech BRIO",
           "error":"Video device not found ... Input/output error"}}

ok:true beside a device-not-found error. The service reported healthy, the panel agreed, and the camera was sitting on the desk unplugged.

Why the existing check could not catch it

one camera each reads the -i <device> argument off the running ffmpeg processes. ffmpeg was running and failing, and its command line still said "Logitech BRIO" — so the check reported what the service intended to open, which is not the same question. It is a check on intent wearing the clothes of a check on outcome.

The new one asks the service what actually happened, and treats a non-empty error as a failure whatever ok claims. It fired immediately:

FAIL  studio-locate camera is really open
      Logitech BRIO reports ok but: stream frozen for 10s; restarted
fix:  the camera is not on the bus — check the cable before restarting anything

Two smaller things, one of them mine

The error text changed between two reads a minute apart — "Video device not found" became "stream frozen for 10s; restarted" — because the service is retrying. Keying on any non-empty error rather than on a particular message is what makes that survivable.

And the cause-reporting added two hours earlier had gone into the service check only. The HTTP check for the same process still said fetch failed — try kickstart: the wrong advice on the same screen as the right one, which is worse than none because it invites the restart loop. Both now read from one map. That is the same partial-fix pattern this session keeps finding, this time in a fix of my own, two hours old. 277 tests, 42 checks.

114A regression pass that found no regression, and one invented detail

Today changed conversations, the deck, the doctor, the jobs, the improve loop, the teacher and the memory writer. That is enough surface to warrant asking the assistant a real question and reading the answer properly.

used: health_report, recent_work

Four health checks are failing... The air trackpad hasn't been
working since 11:31am today... Studio locate camera is not open...
Outlook and push have been down since yesterday afternoon.

I handled 7 requests in the last 24 hours.

All four failures named, durations right, both of the new self-knowledge tools used unprompted, and the request count is correct — 7 is the non-probe traffic, so the probe filtering is holding end to end. No regression from a day of changes.

One sentence in it is wrong

It described the airpad as "the touch-sensitive surface on your desk setup". It is an air trackpad — hand tracking through a webcam, with nothing to touch.

The interesting part is where that came from. The tool output it was reading says, verbatim, NOT TRUE: "com.cleetus.airpad (the air trackpad)". The source was correct and present. This is not the missing-source failure the last several sections have been about — it is elaboration past the evidence: taking a correct three-word label and confidently expanding it into a description of hardware that does not exist.

Left unfixed, on purpose

Every other invention found this session had a fix in the shape of a missing source: no tool could answer, so one was built. That lever does not exist here. The remaining options are a prompt line telling it not to embellish — of uncertain value, since it already carries "if you do not know something, say so" and this did not feel like not knowing — or writing a hardware description into his MEMORY.md, which would be my inference about his equipment installed permanently in his memory. Neither is clearly better than recording it accurately and leaving the decision to him. 277 tests, 42 checks, 10s per doctor pass.

115Which tools have never once been used

The check that found pre-event-brief asked whether a job had ever done anything. The same question one level down: of 38 tools, how many have ever been called? The run files record every tool call, so this is countable rather than guessable.

tools: 38   ever called: 27

never called: clone_repo, detect, forget_secret, get_secret,
              github, learn_face, save_secret, save_skill,
              send_email, watch, web_act

Eleven of thirty-eight. Most of those are correct and worth stating so the list is not mistaken for a defect report: send_email is deliberately unused — the handoff has said since it was built that the first one should be his — and clone_repo, github, save_secret and forget_secret all write something, so never having fired is what a careful assistant looks like. save_skill is called by the teacher directly rather than through the agent loop, so it would never appear here at all.

Three that needed checking

web_act is the one that mattered: the browser can open pages and read them, and the tool that acts on them had never once run. It works — verified on a real page across its safe actions:

web_act(read  ): Example Domain — things you can act on: [0] Learn more
web_act(scroll): Example Domain — things you can act on: [0] Learn more
web_act(back  ): (untitled) — about:blank

And its failure mode is honest. Called with no page open it answered "The browser refused that: mouse.wheel: Target page, context or browser has been closed" — the real reason, from the browser, rather than a silent no-op.

Two left unverified, and it is worth being plain about it

watch and detect both need a desk camera and neither camera is on the USB bus, so there was no way to exercise them. They are the tools most likely to be quietly broken — never called, and depending on hardware that has been absent all afternoon. Writing a test that mocks the camera would prove the dispatch works and nothing about whether the tool does its job, which is the kind of green this session keeps taking apart. They stay on the list. 277 tests, 42 checks.

116The tool that stopped working, and nothing said so

Section 115 counted the tools that had never once been called. This is the failure that count cannot see: a tool that is being called, every time, and failing every time.

search_files and find_files were both written against ripgrep. Ripgrep left this machine on 14 August, sometime between 11:21 and 15:30 — the run files fix both ends of that window, a real search at 11:21 and nothing but errors afterwards. There is no Homebrew record of it ever having been installed: no Cellar directory, no linked keg. Something removed it and nothing announced it.

From that moment both tools answered the same way to every question asked of them:

search failed: Command failed: rg --line-number --no-heading ...
find failed: spawn rg ENOENT

Why it was invisible

The disguise here was better than usual. rg still resolves when a human types it, because the terminal defines it as a shell function rather than a program on disk. So which rg prints something, typing rg foo works, and every way a person would check by hand says the tool is fine — while every spawn from the daemon gets ENOENT, because a shell function does not exist as far as execFile is concerned.

The only trace was one line inside a run file, and run files are not read unless you already suspect something. Cleetus himself did better than the monitoring did: on 12 August he hit the failure, guessed at a tool called shell that does not exist, then reached run_shell and did the search with grep by hand. He got the user their answer. It cost him three tool calls and a wrong guess, and it never surfaced anywhere as a problem.

What changed

Both tools now fall back to grep and find, which are in the base system and cannot go missing. Slower than ripgrep, without the smart-case and gitignore handling — and a slower search that runs beats a faster one that is not there. Verified end to end through the running daemon, not just in the test suite:

POST /chat  "which file under cleetusd/test contains onABareMachine?"
→ used: ['search_files']   answer: nooptionalbinaries.test.mjs

The check that would have caught it

The fallback is not the lesson. The lesson is that a tool he depends on can stop working and nothing notices, so the doctor now calls both tools against a directory known to contain the answer and reads what comes back. Inspecting config would have agreed with itself and proved nothing; this fails the moment the tool does.

cleetusd · his search tools still run
  both answered correctly on a known directory

Six tests cover it, and the sweep among them is the general form: every tool that shells out to a binary outside the base system must handle that binary being absent. It was proven to fail before it was believed — a fake unhandled spawn was injected and the test went red, then the file was restored. Today two tools depend on ripgrep and both handle its absence; if a third arrives without that handling, the suite says so by name.

Still open and unchanged: both physical webcams remain off the USB bus — only the OBS virtual camera and the iPhone are visible — so the cable is still Grayson's to check. Related: studio-locate reports "Logitech BRIO reports ok" while no BRIO is attached, which means it is naming the device from its configuration rather than from what it actually opened. The doctor catches it anyway, by way of the frozen stream rather than the wrong name.

117Counting which tools actually work

Section 115 counted the tools that had never been called. Section 116 was a tool being called constantly and failing constantly — which that count cannot see. This is the check for the second shape, asked of all thirty-eight at once.

The evidence was already on disk. Every tool call and its result is written into the run files as it happens, so how often each tool actually works is countable rather than guessable. Nothing was doing the counting. src/toolhealth.mjs now does, over a three-day window, and the doctor reports any tool that failed every call it received.

cleetusd · no tool is failing every time it is called
  28 tools used across 100 runs, worst is web_read 1/1

The whole difficulty is one distinction

"No matches for kayak"   — the tool RAN. Nothing was there. Healthy.
"search failed: ..."     — the tool could not run at all. Broken.

Get it backwards in one direction and the check screams on every honest empty result until someone stops reading it; get it backwards in the other and it stays quiet through exactly the outage it exists for. So neither list is inferred from keywords — both are written out, and the healthy list is consulted first. A false positive here costs more than a miss, because an ignored check still looks like coverage.

Three rules that came from being wrong first

The first version reported read_file as broken. It was reading doctor.mjs, which is full of strings like "not answering" and "could not" because that is what this file is about. So: a failure must be short (a tool that cannot run says one line, not six hundred characters), it must appear near the front of the line rather than buried mid-sentence, and for tools that return arbitrary content — read_file, vault_search, run_shell — it must open the result or it does not count. run_shell is in that list for a second reason: a command exiting non-zero is the model's command failing, not the tool.

Bad paths are also deliberately not failures. No such file: means the model passed a path that is not there, and a check that fires on it is reporting typos as an outage.

What it says about right now

shell         4/123   No such tool: shell        (fixed 12 Aug — aliases)
search_files  2/9     search failed: ...         (fixed 14 Aug — section 116)
cloud_api     2/35    Could not get Schwab token (needs re-auth)
web_open      2/8     browser harness not running (it is running now)

Nothing is currently failing every time. Every entry above is either already fixed or a known integration waiting on Grayson — and that was worth establishing, because up to now the only way to learn it was to read a hundred run files by hand, which is precisely how section 116 stayed hidden.

Its limit, stated rather than discovered later: it only sees tools somebody used. A tool that breaks and then goes untouched for a week is invisible here — which is why it sits beside the check that calls the search tools directly rather than replacing it. Nine tests cover it, including the two that pull against each other, and the doctor check was proven to fail before it was believed: a fake run file showing a tool dead on every call turned it red, and it went green again when the file was removed.

118The improve loop was telling the truth about what it did, and not about what happened

Its log said it had attempted everything it knew about, which reads like a loop that has finished its work. What it actually meant was that two checks are still failing and it has stopped trying to fix them.

The loop has run eight times and shipped twice, both on 14 August, both to functions/api/health.js:

5ab77bb  if (!r.ok) return null;     — a real bug. The brief check is green now.
3909977  "phone registered but no push has been sent yet"
                                     — a better MESSAGE. push is exactly as down as it was.

Nothing can fix push from here; the phone has to open the app. The loop knew that — it computes stillFailing a few lines before it writes the record, and already trusted that value enough to withhold writing a skill. Then it wrote down the same word for both commits: shipped. That is the word someone reads months later, and it is the difference between a fix and a change.

shipped                                  → 5ab77bb
shipped, but push is still failing       → 3909977

The sentence that made idleness look like success

The second one mattered more. Every pass since then has ended with:

nothing new — all 4 known issues have been attempted

That describes the loop's own bookkeeping. It says nothing about the machine, and what it hides is that all four are still there. Retiring them is correct — two of them wait on Grayson and no amount of code will move them — but retiring them quietly is how an idle loop comes to look like a healthy one, which is the failure this entire file exists to catch.

Where the first rewrite was wrong

The obvious fix — call all four "still failing" — is false, and the dry run caught it before it shipped. Only two of the four are live: health: keys are read fresh from /api/health. The other two are log: keys scraped from cleetusd.err.log, and that file never forgets. The ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT lines in it were fixed and verified on 13 August; they will sit in that log forever, be re-found on every pass, and can never clear. Describing them as current failures would have been exactly the kind of confident wrong sentence this loop keeps getting caught writing.

2 checks are still failing and already attempted: outlook, push;
2 older log/run records that cannot clear on their own

Both sentences were lifted out of improveOnce into idleSummary() and shipOutcome() so they can be tested at all — everything around them does git and network work, which is how they went unexamined long enough to drift. Seven tests, one of them asserting the summary can never again claim convergence. Suite at 305.

Checked and found already handled, so it is not worth re-investigating: the loop commits with git add -A, which would sweep up any uncommitted work in ~/cleetusv2 and take it along on a revert — but a dirty-tree guard refuses to run at all in that case, and the tree was clean. The retirement rule likewise turned out to be deliberate, with a seven-day cooldown and a recovered-since test, not an oversight.

119A worklist that never shrank, and the model going missing while it did

The open-loops file said five things were unfinished. One was. The other four had been re-run and answered days earlier by the improve loop, which wrote "verified fixed" into a state file that nothing else reads.

- FAILED · What am I doing in this picture?
- FAILED ×2 · can you fix studio locate
- FAILED ×2 · how much can you actually edit your own code
- FAILED · learn all the stuff in here and add the skills
- FAILED · search my obsidian vault for anything about breakouts

Every one of those is real: the runs did fail, ending mid-sentence on "Let me check…". But a run file records status: failed once and keeps it forever, so a question sits on this list for seven days regardless of what happens next. Meanwhile improve-state.json held six entries keyed run:<filename> — three "verified fixed by re-running the original question", three "cause already fixed". Two systems knew different things and neither told the other. Grayson reads the worklist; nothing reads the state file.

# Open loops 2026-08-14

- FAILED · What am I doing in this picture? One sentence.

6 earlier failures were re-run and answered since, so they are not listed.

The one that survives is genuinely open — it needs a camera, and the cameras are down. The footer is deliberate: an item vanishing silently from a worklist is its own small betrayal, and the count is what makes a suddenly shorter list believable.

Resolution is matched narrowly. "Shipped" is not resolution — section 118 is precisely that case — so anything whose outcome mentions still failing, gates failing, or a revert is excluded by name rather than by hoping the word "fixed" is absent. And a missing or unparseable state file returns an empty set, so the worst case is the old behaviour where everything stays listed. Never the reverse: a parse error must not quietly empty his worklist.

Ollama was gone

Found while running the suite, not while looking for it: test/plumbing.test.mjs failed on ollama unreachable: fetch failed. No process, no API, and com.cleetus.ollama was not merely stopped — it had been unloaded from launchd entirely, which is why KeepAlive never brought it back. The plist and the binary were both fine and the log ended on "all slots are idle", so nothing crashed. It was still running at 15:40 with PID 70858.

launchctl bootstrap gui/$(id -u) ~/Library/LaunchAgents/com.cleetus.ollama.plist
→ qwen2.5vl:7b, lfm2.5:8b, laguna-xs-2.1:q8_0
→ "Yes, I'm running locally on your Mac Studio using laguna-xs-2.1:q8_0 through Ollama."

Worth saying plainly, because it is the opposite of most entries in this document: the monitoring worked. The chat job failed loudly the moment the model went away — "cleetusd answers but reports unhealthy: ollama unreachable" — and the doctor's scheduled jobs are working check went red and named it. The heartbeat had simply last run at 17:13, before the outage began. Nothing was hidden; it just had not come round yet.

Timing worth recording rather than concluding from: Homebrew's Cellar was modified at 15:16 today, ripgrep vanished from this machine in the same window (section 116), and the ollama agent was unloaded some time after 15:40. Three events, one afternoon, no proven common cause — but the next person who finds a service quietly missing should look at what Homebrew did that day. Suite at 313.

120Stopped and unloaded are not the same kind of gone

The check that would have caught yesterday's outage in seconds did not exist, and the two checks that came closest both passed the whole time it was happening.

When com.cleetus.ollama disappeared, the doctor was already asking two careful questions about launch agents: does every agent's program still exist on disk, and does every plist parse as XML. Both were green. They had to be — the plist was there, the binary was there, and the log ended on "all slots are idle". Nothing had broken. The agent had simply been unloaded.

That is a different kind of gone. KeepAlive only applies to an agent launchd knows about, so an unloaded agent is not a service that is down — it is a service that no longer exists as far as the system is concerned. Nothing was going to bring it back, and nothing did.

services · every launch agent is loaded into launchd
  all 18 agents loaded

Why this is not the "is it running" check that was deliberately avoided

The existing comment in doctor.mjs is explicit that these checks are about the PATH and not about whether a job is running, and that reasoning is still right: most of these agents are scheduled, so sitting idle with a dash for a PID is their healthy state. A check on "running" would light up on every run and be ignored inside a week.

"Loaded" is different in kind, and the distinction is the whole check. A loaded agent that is idle will fire at its next interval. An unloaded one never will. Two of the seven tests exist only to pin that down — an agent with no PID is loaded, and an agent with a -9 exit status is loaded — because the moment those get treated as missing, the check becomes noise and stops being read.

The fix text names the right command

launchctl bootstrap gui/$(id -u) ~/Library/LaunchAgents/<label>.plist
— kickstart does nothing for an agent that is not loaded

That is in the check rather than in someone's memory because the instinct on seeing a dead service is kickstart, and kickstart against a label launchd has never heard of fails with "Could not find service" — which reads like the service is broken rather than absent, and sends you looking in the wrong place. That is exactly what happened yesterday before the right command was found.

Matching is exact rather than substring, so com.apple.ollama in the listing cannot mask a missing com.cleetus.ollama. Unreadable launchctl output reports every agent as missing rather than none — silence there would recreate the original outage precisely. Verified against real launchctl list output with one deliberately absent label, so the parsing is tested against the real format and not against a fixture that agrees with it. Suite at 320, live doctor at 50 checks.

Down to three failing, and all three are the same two physical problems: the airpad and studio-locate both want cameras that are not on the USB bus, and plaid, outlook and push are all waiting on Grayson. Ollama has stayed up since it was restored, and all eighteen agents are loaded.

121Asked what it did wrong, shown only what it was asked
